As $\lambda\to0$, the ceiling trivially satisfies everyone including the extreme firms, so $h(e)$ stops responding to $\lambda$ for anyone. Two tests proposed at the time (firm-exclusion, concave-$q$ derivative comparison) — both later run, see above, and both confirmed this mechanism (trim decisively, concave-$q$ only marginally).
- **How the CI was actually constructed, clarified for the user same day:** genuine test inversion, not a shortcut. `fit_one_lambda_A` already profiles the nuisance vector via BOBYQA at each fixed $\lambda$ (the restricted-model optimum); `min(Lhat)` across the grid is the unrestricted optimum (every point already profiled); reject if $2n\hat Q_n(\lambda)>\chi^2_{8,0.95}$ — literally Chernozhukov-Hong-Tamer/Theorem F.1, mechanically identical to Schennach's own Fig S.2/S.3 and AK2020's `F_figure1.R`. One honest caveat: CI bounds are the literal min/max of *tested* grid points passing, not a continuous root-solve — precise where the grid is dense (upper bound), not a coarseness artifact where it isn't (the lower bound, genuinely flat).

**Firm-exclusion robustness check confirms the mechanism, cleanly (2026-09-05).** Added `trim_top_pct` to `build_run_sample`/`run_stage2_elvis_AB` (CLI-exposed, default 0) — drops the top share of **interior** (non-corner) firms by $M^*$; corner firms untouched (structurally $e\equiv0$, can't be "extreme evaders"). Design: 4 trim levels (0.1%, 0.5%, 1%, 5%) × both `ins`, a small 6-point widely-log-spaced $\lambda$ grid (`5e-9,1.8e-8,1e-7,1e-6,1e-5,1e-4`) — a fast directional diagnostic, not a publication-grade CI at each level. All 8 runs completed (launched overnight, ran autonomously):

| trim | lag_m argmin | lag_m $\hat L_n$ | lag_2_cal_W argmin | lag_2_cal_W $\hat L_n$ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 (untrimmed) | 1.8e-08 | 3.0e-05 | 1.8e-08 | 3.7e-05 |
| 0.1% (30 firms) | 1e-07 | 0.0060 | 1e-07 | 0.00063 |
| 0.5% (146 firms) | 1e-07 | 0.0015 | 1e-07 | 0.0037 |
| 1% (291 firms) | 1e-06 | 0.0144 | 1e-07 | 0.0059 |
| 5% (1452 firms) | 1e-06 | 0.0246 | 1e-06 | 0.0469 |

Every fit shows a genuine bracketed minimum within the 6-point grid — the flat plateau is gone the moment even the top 0.1% (30 of 29,038 firms) is removed. Argmin climbs monotonically with trimming, roughly two orders of magnitude across the range, landing back near CLAUDE.md's historical pre-extension $\lambda\sim10^{-6}$ estimates. $\hat L_n$ also degrades substantially with trimming (worse fit) — expected: the untrimmed model's near-perfect fit at $\lambda\to0$ was very plausibly an artifact of a few extreme-$e$ firms making $h(e)$ trivially insensitive to $\lambda$ for nearly everyone, not a genuinely well-identified population parameter. Honest limitation: still the coarse 6-point diagnostic, not a full Phase-0-style sweep per trim level — argmins precise to about an order of magnitude. This result, plus the concave-$q$ rejection (above), led directly to the 1% trim-cutoff decision detailed under "Stage-2 ELVIS coding sessions" above. **This 1% decision was later revised down to 0.5% — see "$h'$ numerical fix and warm-start prototyping" and "Trim re-check with the fixed $h'$ moment" below (2026-09-07).**

## $h'$ numerical fix and warm-start prototyping (2026-09-07)

**Starting point: user's own prototype in `1205-stage2-warmstart.R` tried z-score standardization of raw $h'$, reviewed for bugs.** Independently of the agreed `exp(h')` plan, the user had experimented with `(h_prime - mean(h_prime))/sd(h_prime)` as the bounding transform, handling the near-zero-denominator case via `ifelse(1-2*lambda*e_hat==0, -9e200, raw_formula)`. Code review found this didn't actually work as intended:
- **The `==0` guard is dead code.** Floating-point equality to exactly 0 essentially never happens for a continuous `lambda`/`e_hat` combination — the real danger is the denominator landing at `~1e-15`, not `0`. In that zone raw `h_prime` returns something like `±1e14`, which is `is.finite()` in R, so it silently passes every check.
- **`lambda_min <- 1/(2*max(e_hat))` sits at an EXACT mathematical singularity of the unclipped formula.** Plugging in: $1-2\lambda_{\min}e_{\max}=1-2\cdot\frac1{2e_{\max}}\cdot e_{\max}=0$ exactly (up to floating-point rounding) — the optimizer's own lower search bound was defined at precisely the point where the most extreme firm-draw's `h_prime` blows up.
- **`h`'s clip (`arg<-pmax(1-2*lambda*e_hat,1e-6)`) and `h_prime`'s denominator (the raw, unclipped expression) disagreed** — meaning `h_prime` could report a large nonzero slope exactly where the clip had already made `h` locally flat in $\lambda$, a derivative inconsistent with the function it's supposed to be the derivative of.

**Fix applied: `h_prime` reuses the SAME clipped `arg` as `h`.** One line (`h_prime <- -2*e_hat/arg`), removes the `ifelse`/sentinel entirely (never divides by less than `1e-6`), and restores derivative consistency. Rerun: `lag_m` still ran ~24 minutes without finishing (killed) — worse, not better, than before the fix. Diagnosed: `1e-6` is a far looser tolerance than the old (never-firing) `==0` check, so the floor now caps a real, non-trivial share of the 7.26M-row sample simultaneously as $\lambda$ moves (each observation has its own threshold $\lambda_i^*$ where it crosses onto the floor) — this changes the objective's shape at many points at once, and with `mean`/`sd` recomputed fresh every `gmm_obj` call, L-BFGS-B's numerical gradient/line search started chasing a moving target.

**Switched the warm-start's transform to `exp(h_prime)` (of the now-floored `h_prime`), not z-score normalization.** Once floored, `h_prime<=0` always (`e>=0`, `arg>0`), so `exp(h_prime)` is bounded in $(0,1]$ — a pointwise transform of one observation's own $e$, no dependence on the rest of the sample. Rerun: `lag_m` converged cleanly in ~4.3 min (`optim`'s `convergence=0`). `lag_2_cal_W` returned `convergence=52` — an L-BFGS-B **line-search failure** (not just hitting `maxit`), still finished (finite output), but not a validated optimum.

**Root cause of the line-search failure, and why it's mostly a warm-start-specific wall.** `arg`'s floor creates a kink at each observation's own crossing-$\lambda$; with ~7.26M distinct values these are effectively continuous kinks across the tested $\lambda$ range. L-BFGS-B's finite-difference gradient can be locally unreliable near enough of them. But the *real* ELVIS estimator never searches $\lambda$ smoothly at all — $\lambda$ is gridded, and the inner BOBYQA optimization runs at one fixed $\lambda$ per call, so the specific mechanism that broke L-BFGS-B here (kinks accumulating *as $\lambda$ varies*) can't recur the same way there. Two things do carry over: (i) BOBYQA is already derivative-free (trust-region quadratic model, no line search) — the real estimator was never as exposed to this failure mode as the warm-start's L-BFGS-B choice; (ii) $\eta$ (the relative-evasion floor, which sets the sampler's support ceiling) is the one inner-problem parameter that could echo a milder version of the same kink, worth watching once the floor lands in the real C++ code, though BOBYQA should handle it better than L-BFGS-B did.

**Switched the warm-start's optimizer to `nloptr::neldermead` (bounded), not `stats::optim(method="Nelder-Mead")`** — base R's Nelder-Mead silently ignores `lower`/`upper` (only `L-BFGS-B`/`Brent` respect bounds in `stats::optim`), which would have quietly stopped enforcing `lambda_min`/`lambda_max`/the delta box. Nelder-Mead never estimates a gradient (compares function values at simplex vertices only), so it has no line search to fail. Rerun (`maxeval=2000` default): both instruments finished without error in ~7.7 min combined — `lag_m` genuinely converged (`NLOPT_XTOL_REACHED`, $\hat\lambda\approx1.817\times10^{-8}$), `lag_2_cal_W` hit the `maxeval` cap (`NLOPT_MAXEVAL_REACHED`, not an error, just not fully converged, $\hat\lambda\approx7.57\times10^{-8}$). A targeted rerun of `lag_2_cal_W` alone with `maxeval=30000` (new `ins_only`/`maxeval_nm` CLI args added to `1205-stage2-warmstart.R`) converged genuinely in only ~7 min / 2725 iterations (`NLOPT_XTOL_REACHED`, $\hat\lambda\approx1.879\times10^{-8}$) — far faster than a naive linear extrapolation from the 2000-eval run suggested, and landing almost exactly on `lag_m`'s own value.

**Cross-check against the real estimator: both warm-start $\hat\lambda$'s land almost exactly on the real, full-scale untrimmed estimator's own Phase-0 finding** ($1.836\times10^{-8}$ for `lag_m`, $1.800\times10^{-8}$ for `lag_2_cal_W`, from the 2026-09-05 continuous-chain sweep). A crude 4-moment GMM warm start, built completely differently (plain pooled mean, no gamma/tilt, no MCMC), landing almost on top of the real 8-moment CUE/BOBYQA/Metropolis machinery's minimum is good evidence the `h'` score moment carries real identifying content once computed soundly, not just numerical noise.

**Ported the same fix into the real C++ estimator.** `Code/Rcpp/1200-stage2-elvis-common.h`: added a shared `H_DENOM_FLOOR=1e-6` and `h_denom(e,lambda)` helper, used by both `h_of_e` and `h_prime_of_e` (flooring `h_of_e` too, not just `h_prime_of_e`, for the same consistency argument — a real behavior change to every `psi`-based moment, though only in the same vanishing near-ceiling sliver, flagged explicitly before applying). `moment_g_A_one`/`moment_g_B_one` (`1200-stage2-elvis.cpp`/`-B.cpp`) changed to `std::exp(h_prime_of_e(e,lambda))` in place of the raw value. Compiled clean via `sourceCpp` for both files. Smoke-tested end-to-end: moment set A (single $\lambda$ point, real sample) converged with `max|gamma|=1.45` (vs. the ~1e16-variance-row collapse the raw version produced); moment set B ran clean too (hit its toy `maxeval` cap as expected at that tiny scale, no NaN/crash).

**Does `draw_from_rho_eta` already prevent the singularity? No — confirmed directly, not just reasoned about.** `u01` comes from `std::uniform_real_distribution<double>(0.0,1.0)`, mathematically `[0,1)` — never returns exactly 1, so the sampler genuinely guarantees $e<1/(2\lambda)$ (or the $\eta$-adjusted bound) strictly, no division by exactly zero. But a continuous uniform draw puts positive density arbitrarily close to `u01=1`, so across enough draws (thousands of firms × `n_burn+n_keep` steps × every BOBYQA evaluation) some land close enough that $1-2\lambda e\sim10^{-14}$ — finite, undetected, still large enough to dominate `cov(Ghat)`'s eigenvalues. Direct proof this isn't just theoretical: the original raw-$h'$ bug (row 6 variance $\sim1.16\times10^{16}$x every other row) was found **with** `draw_from_rho_eta` already active in the sampler — the sampler fix and the floor fix solve two different problems (legal support vs. bounded magnitude within that support), both needed.

**Is the floor a numerical fix only, or does it change the theoretical estimates?** Judged fundamentally numerical: the floor only ever activates in the sliver where $1-2\lambda e<10^{-6}$, and in that sliver the *true* $h(e)$ already diverges to $-\infty$ — there's no finite ground truth for a floor to distort, since any floating-point implementation has to pick *some* way to represent that limit (the uncontrolled alternative, raw hardware rounding, isn't more faithful, just less controlled). Doesn't touch $\theta$'s definition or the population moment conditions. One open, cheap-to-run robustness check flagged but not yet done: refit with a tighter floor (e.g. `1e-8`/`1e-10`) and confirm $\hat\lambda$/the CI don't move — if stable across floor choices, confirms it's purely numerical; if not, the floor is doing quiet economic work and needs more care.

## Trim re-check with the fixed $h'$ moment (2026-09-07)

**Question reopened: does the now-bounded `exp(h')` score moment remove the NEED to trim, or just the numerical corruption it was causing?** Hypothesis going in (later borne out): probably still need to trim. The original flat-plateau finding that motivated the 1% decision was established using the *old* 8-moment system, before the `h'` score moment existed at all (that slot held `eps*e`) — so it's a property of `h(e)` itself (used in `psi`, most of the other rows), not the new moment's numerics. And `h'(e)\to-2e$ as $\lambda\to0$ (regardless of transform) still scales with $e$, so distinguishing small candidate $\lambda$'s still needs firms with large enough $e$ — `exp(h')` is a *monotonic* transform of `h'` alone, so it can bound how much one draw's raw magnitude dominates the CUE weighting matrix, but doesn't change *which* firms carry the identifying signal.

**Design: 5 trim levels (0%, 0.1%, 0.5%, 1%, 5%) instead of the original 11, both instruments, full 16-point $\lambda$ grid ($10^{-9}$ to $10^{-4}$), real settings (`n_burn=500,n_keep=1000`)** — smoke-tested first at toy scale (confirmed the cross-trim warm-start chaining, including the never-before-explicitly-tested `trim_top_pct=0` case, works cleanly), then launched at real scale via `run-trim-sequential.sh`, both instruments sequentially on one machine (not concurrently, to avoid oversubscribing 12 cores with two `n_cores=10` jobs at once).

**Result: the old pathology (flat, never-turns-around plateau as $\lambda\to0$) is gone for BOTH instruments** — every trim level, both instruments, now shows a genuine bracketed minimum (all 80 grid points converged, `conv=1` throughout, verified not assumed). That part of the hypothesis was right.

**But the clean multi-level stabilization turned out to be instrument-specific, not automatic.** `lag_m` locks onto $\hat\lambda\approx2.15\times10^{-5}$ across three consecutive levels (0.5%, 1%, 5%) — argmins $4.64\times10^{-7}$ (0%), $4.64\times10^{-8}$ (0.1%, actually *smaller* than untrimmed), then $2.15\times10^{-5}$, $2.15\times10^{-5}$, $2.15\times10^{-5}$ (0.5%/1%/5%). `lag_2_cal_W` instead sits at a much smaller, flatter plateau around $\lambda\approx10^{-6}$ through 0%, 0.1%, 0.5% (three levels agreeing with *each other*, not with `lag_m`), a broad weakly-resolved trough at 1% ($\lambda\approx4.64\times10^{-7}$, nearly tied across a decade of the grid), and only moves toward `lag_m`'s territory at 5% ($10^{-5}$, about half of `lag_m`'s value) via a sharp jump rather than a gradual convergence. Consistent with (not contradicting) this project's repeated prior finding that `lag_2_cal_W` behaves less stably than `lag_m`.

**Built `Code/Deconvolution/1222-stage2-trim-all-gridpoints-plot.R`** (fills a gap — the original all-gridpoints figure, `Paper/images/1223-stage2-trim-all-gridpoints.png` from 2026-09-05, was generated ad hoc with no saved script) — plots every tested grid point, test-inversion pass/fail ($TS=2n[\hat L_n(\lambda)-\min_{\lambda'}\hat L_n(\lambda')]$ vs. $\chi^2_{8,0.95}=15.51$) rather than just a summary CI band, trim on a discrete equally-spaced axis (a continuous axis was tried and rejected the first time this kind of figure was made — it bunches everything near zero). Output: `Paper/images/1222-stage2-trim-all-gridpoints-refixed.png`, raw table `Code/Products/1222-stage2-trim-all-gridpoints.csv`.

**Decision: trim cutoff REVISED from 1% to 0.5%**, reading the criterion off the 95% CI's **upper bound** across levels (per the user's own read of the figure), not the point estimate. For `lag_m`, the upper bound jumps between 0.1% and 0.5%, then holds flat across 0.5%/1%/5% (small further jump at 5%) — 0.5% is the first level where it stabilizes. For `lag_2_cal_W`, the upper bound is broadly stable from 0% through 5% except a one-off widening exactly at 1% (visible in the figure as a much wider spread of passing points at that level) — since 0.5% itself isn't affected by that widening, it doesn't argue against 0.5% as a safe choice. Both instruments are consistent with 0.5%; neither points at needing the heavier 1% cut. Same underlying investigation as the original 1% decision (extreme-evader leverage on $\lambda$ under linear $q$, firm/industry composition sanity checks) — only the specific cutoff value changed, not the reasoning behind trimming at all.

**Not yet done:** a full Phase-0-style dense/continuous-chain sweep + closed-form test-inversion CI *at* the 0.5% cutoff specifically (this revision used the coarser 16-point grid across 5 trim levels, not Phase 0's original fine sweep) — needed for a precise $\hat\lambda$/CI to actually feed the counterfactual, not just to confirm the cutoff choice. The counterfactual's Phase 1 remains blocked on this.

## Standalone $(\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ grid estimator: built, calibrated, then paused in favor of moment set A (2026-09-07/08)

**Motivation:** the 0.5%-trim moment-set-A fit came back with $\hat\delta_2<0$ for both instruments — wrong sign for the U-shaped cost story. Per the "grid parameters of interest jointly, profile true nuisance" rule, decided to grid $(\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ directly (profiling $\delta_0$) rather than trust a single black-box inner optimization, to see the actual shape of the objective surface and whether $\delta_2>0$ is genuinely rejected.

**Built a standalone C++ binary** (`Code/C-estimator/`), not another Rcpp module, on the premise that eliminating R-interpreter overhead between BOBYQA calls would meaningfully speed this up. Along the way:
- **`h'` transform corrected a second time.** The first fix (`exp(h')`, from the earlier session) turned out to underflow to exactly `0.0` for ~73-75% of firms (median $e\approx841\Rightarrow h'\approx-1683$, deep in `exp`'s underflow zone) — silently gutting the score moment for most of the sample, not a numerical nicety. Replaced with $h'/(1-h')$ (bounded $(-1,0]$, decays polynomially not exponentially, stays numerically distinguishable across the full range of $e$) — implemented once as `h_prime_bounded` in the shared `1200-stage2-elvis-common.h`, live in moment sets A, B, and the (now-paused) grid estimator's set C simultaneously. Corner-firm handling corrected to match: `h_prime_bounded(0,\cdot)=0` exactly (the old `exp(0)=1` value was inconsistent with A/B's own established "leave this row at 0 for corner firms" convention).
- **Moment set C, built and then found wanting.** Started at the user's proposed 6 rows (psi, psi·om, psi·om², eps·h', psi·eps, psi·eps·om) — dropped ALL of A's eps-only rows, so corner firms (10.36% of the trimmed sample) contributed exactly zero to every row. Added raw `eps` back as a 7th row (gradually, one at a time). $\hat L_n$ values from this reduced set came back substantially higher than moment set A's — a real signal the reduction cost real fit quality, not just corner-firm identification, reinforcing the eventual decision to abandon it (see below).
- **R vs. C++ speed: no gain at this scale.** Single-threaded, R/Rcpp and the C++ binary are identical (1.72s vs 1.73s per CUE-objective call at `n_burn=500,n_keep=2000`; `dvec` bit-identical). The premise was wrong for this regime — R's per-call overhead mattered a lot back when moment set B was built at `n_burn=100,n_keep=1000` (R's `cov()` was ~95% of an ~18ms call then), but at 500/2000 raw MCMC compute dominates so heavily that marshalling overhead is noise. What actually mattered was per-firm threading, which the standalone build initially lacked — added via `std::thread` (mirroring RcppParallel's split) plus a shell-level shard mechanism (`run_grid_shards.sh`).
- **Amdahl's law fit from real measurements: serial fraction $f\approx5\%$.** From $T_1=1.73$s, $T(4\text{ threads})=109.0$s, $T(12\text{ threads})=49.0$s per point (same 101 evals both times, deterministic). Implied pure task-parallelism ($S=12$ single-threaded shards) is throughput-optimal — confirmed no contention penalty running concurrent single-threaded shards. This was a genuine, useful result, but see below for the gap it had.
- **Settled: `n_burn=500,n_keep=2000` is necessary, `250/1000` is not an adequate substitute even for exploration.** Same grid point, both genuinely converged (`conv=1`, not a `maxtime` artifact): $\hat L_n$ differed by ~2x (0.061 vs 0.113), $\hat\eta$ by ~36%, several $\gamma$ by an order of magnitude. Real, substantive sensitivity to MCMC noise level, not optimizer noise.
- **Progressive random point sampling (with a persistent "already-run" tracker) was designed, then abandoned in favor of Chebyshev-shell center-out expansion** before ever being run — random sampling has no natural "nearby already-solved point" to warm-start from, reintroducing the exact path-dependence problem the trim sweep already taught this project to avoid (naive/reset warm starts produced 2-4 orders of magnitude of spurious noise there). Center-out on the lattice guarantees a genuine neighbor is already solved before each new point is attempted.
- **Shell-mode built:** center solved via multi-start (3 genuinely different starting points, best kept — since a bad center would silently propagate outward through every chained point, looking stable while being consistently wrong); shells 1..$d$ processed in Chebyshev-distance order, each point warm-started from its nearest already-solved neighbor (log-$\lambda$ + linear $\delta_1,\delta_2$ distance), shell-wise dynamic thread allocation.
- **First real 27-point ($1+26$, shell 0+1) run at `n_burn=500,n_keep=2000`, `threads_per_point` dynamic (1 for the 26-point shell): only 7 of 27 points (26%) genuinely converged** (`conv=1`, not `maxtime`-capped) — the 90s→300s `maxtime` inherited from earlier smoke tests was far too tight once shell-1 batched down to 1 thread/point (single-threaded convergence takes meaningfully longer in wall-clock, even though total compute is the same). Test-inversion on just those 7 converged points ($d_g=7$, $\chi^2_{7,0.95}=14.07$): only the argmin itself passed, everything else (including the center) rejected by 500-6000+ — not read as a real finding, almost certainly a symptom of the tiny/unreliable converged sample, not the true surface shape.
- **Added real diagnostics** before rerunning: per-point wall-clock time (not just cumulative), full pass-1+pass-2 iteration/convergence accounting, and an explicit `threads_per_point` override (the earlier "pure $S=12$" throughput conclusion assumed points always converge — it never accounted for a fixed `maxtime` interacting with convergence reliability, a real gap once single-threaded points needed far longer wall-clock to hit the same eval count). A second 27-point run at `threads_per_point=2` and generous `maxtime=1200` was in progress (center fully converged genuinely, 97-671 evals/pass, 126-255s/point at 12 threads) when the session redirected — killed before shell 1 finished, not wasted (the calibration data already gathered — real per-point timings, real iteration counts — is preserved in the `.Rout` log even though the run itself was stopped).

**Decision: paused, not abandoned outright — moment set A is the active path again, only swapping one row.** $\hat L_n$ from the reduced moment set C came back substantially worse than moment set A's fits; moment set A's three eps-only rows give corner firms real identifying content the counterfactual will need once firms start transitioning into the corner as $\Delta$ varies; and moment set A already has reference estimates ($\hat\delta_1,\hat\delta_2$ in economically sensible ranges, $\hat\lambda$ around $10^{-5}$) to compare a new grid against. The one thing moment set A didn't have until today: its `h'` row already uses `h_prime_bounded` (fixed in the SAME edit that fixed the standalone build's version, since both draw from the shared header) — so "going back to moment set A" is not undoing today's core fix, just dropping the 6/7-row reduction and the standalone-C++ detour.

**Plan for next session:** (1) grid $\lambda$ alone on moment set A (reusing `fit_one_lambda_A`'s existing structure, nothing new to build) — coarse then fine, compare against the known reference estimates; (2) grid $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ alone at the best $\lambda$ from (1) — coarse then fine (this needs a small new piece: `1211-stage2-elvis-driver-AB.R` currently only grids $\lambda$, not $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ at fixed $\lambda$); (3) use the results of (1) and (2) as an *informed* center — not an arbitrary grid-midpoint — before attempting anything like the full joint 3-D exploration again. The `Code/C-estimator/` shell/chaining/threading infrastructure is validated and reusable (real, working engineering) if a joint grid is wanted later with moment set A's structure ported in — just not the next step.

## Moment set A extended to 9 rows; naive-chain local-optimum trap found and worked around; two tollgates (2026-09-08)

**Row [6] restored to `eps*e`, `h_prime_bounded*eps` ADDED as a new row [8] (not a replacement)** — `Code/Rcpp/1200-stage2-elvis.cpp` (`D_G_A` 8→9), `1211-stage2-elvis-driver-AB.R` (gamma-length bounds/init 8→9 throughout). Motivation: the 8-row version (`h_prime_bounded*eps` swapped in for `eps*e`, 2026-09-05) returned $\hat\delta_1,\hat\delta_2<0$ for both instruments at every trim level checked (0.5%, 1%) — wrong sign. The pre-swap 8-row list (this session's restored row [6]) was the one that gave the sensible-sign headline table in `650-stage2-prelim-results.qmd`/`Paper/images/1212-stage2-A-lhat-lambda.png`. Per the user's own read: dropping a moment was the wrong fix for weak $\lambda$ identification (moment set C's rejection, above, already showed that); the fix is to add, not drop — check moments in one at a time and watch how $\hat\theta$ moves.

**Naive ascending warm-start chain found to be trapped in a bad local optimum for `lag_2_cal_W`, confirmed via multi-start.** At trim=0.5%, 9-moment A: `lag_m`'s naive chain (each $\lambda$ point warm-started from the *previous* $\lambda$ point's own fit, starting from that instrument's own GMM warmstart file) landed on a sharp interior minimum with $\hat\delta_1,\hat\delta_2>0$ ($\lambda=1\times10^{-6}$, $\hat L_n=0.00249$). `lag_2_cal_W`'s naive chain instead found $\hat\delta_1,\hat\delta_2<0$ everywhere on its own 16-point grid, with only a shallow, barely-bracketed minimum. A 3-start$\times$3-$\lambda$ diagnostic (naive chain vs. `lag_m`'s own converged par used verbatim as $x_0$ vs. a random $x_0$, saved for reproducibility) showed `lag_m`'s fit finds a **materially better and sign-flipped** optimum at $\lambda=2.15\times10^{-8}$ and $10^{-7}$ specifically (e.g. $\hat L_n$ 0.00524→0.00431, $\hat\delta_1$ flips $-2.44\to+4.16$) — genuine evidence of a local-optimum trap in the naive chain, not that `lag_2_cal_W`'s true surface lacks the sensible-sign region.

**Tried a global optimizer (`NLOPT_GN_DIRECT_L`) before accepting the smarter-seed fix, specifically to have a defensible answer to "did you try a global optimizer first?"** Full 13-dim $(\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2,\eta,\gamma_{1:9})$ vector, $\gamma$ boxed at $\pm50$ for the global pass only (pass 2 stays the existing unbounded local BOBYQA), `maxeval=1000`/`maxtime=300s` per $\lambda$. **Result: DIRECT_L did materially WORSE than either baseline at all 3 test $\lambda$'s** ($\hat L_n\approx0.090$ at every $\lambda$, vs. 0.0043–0.0098 for the naive chain and 0.0043–0.019 for the `lag_m`-seeded fits) — a textbook curse-of-dimensionality failure (DIRECT's box-partitioning needs far more evaluations to resolve a narrow basin as dimension grows; all the objective's identifying weight concentrated on 2 of 9 $\gamma$'s, $\delta_1\approx0$, a degenerate fit). `CRS2_LM` was the other nloptr candidate considered but not run once DIRECT_L's failure, plus the `lag_m`-seed success, was judged sufficient evidence.

**TOLLGATE 1 (settled for this session): start every $\lambda$-grid point from the SAME good, already-validated point, rather than chaining sequentially — chaining reintroduces path dependence.** Discovered the hard way: re-seeding `lag_2_cal_W`'s 16-point coarse grid via the driver's `par_init` CLI override reproduced the naive chain's results **bit-for-bit** (confirmed, `max abs diff = 0`) — `par_init` only ever seeds the grid's *first* point under sequential chaining, so changing it has zero effect on anything downstream. The fix that actually worked: every $\lambda$ point fit **independently** from the identical seed (`lag_m`'s best-fit par), no chaining across $\lambda$ at all (only the existing within-point 2-pass BOBYQA refinement still applies). Ported from the 3-point diagnostic to the full 16-point grid this way for `lag_2_cal_W` (`1235-stage2-lag2W-allpoints-lagmseed.R`) — $\hat\delta_1,\hat\delta_2>0$ at all 16 points, $\hat L_n$ lower than the naive chain almost everywhere. **Side effect flagged in advance by the user and confirmed:** the passing (χ²) region widens substantially under this seeding — 9 of 16 points on `lag_2_cal_W`'s own grid now pass, and a lower-$\lambda$ extension check (1e-10, 1e-12, 1e-14, same independent seeding) found no turnaround at all, flat down to 1e-14. Read as a real, structural finding (low detection probability is consistent with the Colombian setting in this period), not something to keep chasing by extending the grid further — the user explicitly capped this line of investigation here.

**A parallel independent-seed check on `lag_m` itself (seeding its own fine grid from its own best-known point, rather than chaining) found an even better point than the one the naive chain had reported as `lag_m`'s "argmin."** $\lambda=6.51\times10^{-7}$: $\hat L_n=0.000699$, vs. the naive chain's reported best ($\lambda=10^{-6}$, $\hat L_n=0.00249$) — about 3.6x lower, confirming the naive chain's own jaggedness (erratic neighbors found earlier in a chained fine grid near $\lambda=10^{-6}$) really was a path-dependence artifact, not real structure in $\hat L_n(\lambda)$.

**TOLLGATE 2 (flagged, not resolved): the "seed from one good point" fix is a practical workaround, not a formal solution — a genuine global optimizer would be needed to close this off rigorously.** DIRECT_L's failure (above) means that's not yet in hand at reasonable compute cost for the full $(\theta,\gamma)$ vector; revisit once at the $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$-grid stage, where each cell is a smaller, more tractable problem (fixing $\delta_1,\delta_2$ removes 2 of 13 dimensions from the free vector) — a natural, cheaper place to test a global-vs-local comparison systematically rather than at 3 hand-picked points.

**Current best estimates (both instruments, 9-moment A, trim=0.5%, `lag_m`-seeded)**, table+figure at `Paper/tbls/1237-stage2-best-estimates.png` / `Paper/images/1237-stage2-estimates-so-far-delta1-delta2.png`, consolidated data `Code/Products/1237-stage2-estimates-so-far.csv`:

| Instrument | $\lambda$ | $\hat\delta_0$ | $\hat\delta_1$ | $\hat\delta_2$ | $\hat\eta$ | $\hat\omega^*$ | $\hat L_n$ |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| $m^*_{it-1}$ | $6.51\times10^{-7}$ | 3.303 | 4.123 | 0.503 | 0.000746 | 4.102 | 0.000699 |
| $\tilde{\mathcal W}_{it-2}$ | $4.64\times10^{-7}$ | 3.155 | 4.210 | 0.527 | 0.000648 | 3.997 | 0.00419 |

Cross-instrument agreement is a genuine specification check, not something engineered in: $\hat\delta$'s within ~5-6% of each other, $\hat\omega^*$ essentially identical (~4.0-4.1), $\lambda$'s within the same order of magnitude — a marked contrast with the naive-chain fits, which disagreed by orders of magnitude in $\lambda$ and had opposite-signed $\delta$'s.

## MCMC diagnostics on the (delta1,delta2) grid: acceptance-rate/psi instability traced to optimizer training length, not sampler noise (2026-09-08)

**Context: the 49-point (delta1,delta2) grid (lag_m, moment set A, trim=0.5%) came back visibly jagged** (Section: earlier same-day entry) — only 5/49 points passed the $\chi^2_9$ test, scattered rather than contiguous, and the 3D surface showed sharp spikes rather than a smooth bowl. Consulted general (non-ELVIS) MCMC literature per the user's request — Geyer (1992), Flegal-Haran-Jones 2008 ("Can We Trust the Third Significant Figure?", `Lit-Papers/FlegalHaranJones2008-ThirdSignificantFigure.pdf`) and its companion Flegal-Jones 2010 (`Lit-Papers/FlegalJones2010-BatchMeansSpectralVariance.pdf`) — both newly downloaded and saved to `Lit-Papers/`. FHJ2008's own practical default: Consistent Batch Means (CBM), $\theta=1/2$, i.e. $b_n=a_n=\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$; their own toy example directly illustrates our exact symptom (same n, two starting values, MCSE 0.016 vs. 11.01 on point estimates of similar scale). Checked Schennach's own GAUSS reference code (`Lit-Papers/ELVIS_code/elvisutil.g`, `avg_mom`) and AK2020's CUDA code (`Appendix_F/cudafunctions/cuda_fastoptim_counter.jl`) for whether either already implements Schennach's ELVIS.pdf p.360 smooth-reweighting device (fix an importance-sampling pool once, no per-trial accept/reject) — **neither does**: Schennach's own applied code runs full per-trial Metropolis-Hastings with CRN (same architecture we already use), AK2020 doesn't even use CRN (fresh `logunif` every call) — so building the p.360 device would be genuinely new work, not a port, and was deprioritized in favor of the cheaper batch-means diagnostic route.

**Built 4 new standalone-C++ diagnostic modes** (`Code/C-estimator/grid_estimator.cpp`, all read an already-fitted point, no re-optimization): `acceptdiag` (per-firm acceptance rate, cross-firm mean/median/variance/percentiles), `psitraj`/`accepttraj` (per-MCMC-step cross-firm-pooled trajectory of $\psi$ or the accept indicator, for retroactive CBM batch-means analysis at any checkpoint), `gammagdiag` (per-firm mean of $\gamma'g$, the tilting-exponent scalar, cross-firm mean/variance).

**Retroactive CBM checkpoint analysis (growing 10%/step, 1000→5000, on the already-computed n_keep=5000 trajectories) found psi's batch-means variance still growing 6-7x across the whole range for all 3 test points** ((2,0.2), (6,0.3), (1,0.4)) — never stabilizing, and the running mean of psi drifted *monotonically* (not just noisily) the entire way, a hallmark of insufficient equilibration. The SAME analysis on the acceptance-rate trajectory was well-behaved (flat mean, MCSE roughly halving over the same range) — acceptance rate alone is a necessary-but-not-sufficient health check; it can look fine while $\psi$ (the quantity that actually matters) is still far from converged.

**IMPORTANT CORRECTION, flagged directly by the user — do not re-derive this as "confounded" again:** an earlier comparison of cross-firm acceptance-rate SD between (6,0.3)'s own $n_{keep}=1000$ fit (SD=0.1405) and its own $n_{keep}=5000$ fit (SD=0.0693, roughly half) was initially dismissed as an artifact of comparing two different optimizer outputs. **That dismissal was wrong in spirit.** Holding gamma artificially fixed and only varying $n_{keep}$ (a clean-looking "controlled" experiment) showed almost no change (SD 0.0687→0.0693) — but that experiment answers a different, less interesting question (does the sampler alone improve with more draws at a FIXED gamma). The real, intended comparison is exactly the "confounded" one: **does a LONGER chain during the ORIGINAL BOBYQA optimization let the optimizer find a gamma under which the chain behaves better** — and it does, decisively. Confirmed a second way with $\gamma'g$ (the tilting-exponent scalar) at (6,0.3): the $n_{keep}=1000$ fit's own gamma gives cross-firm variance 33.60 (SD 5.80); the $n_{keep}=5000$ fit's own gamma gives variance 10.25 (SD 3.20) — a 3.3x reduction, evaluated at each fit's own training length. Mechanism: parameters *cause* chain behavior (acceptance-rate stability, $\gamma'g$ variance); getting good parameters requires the optimizer to see a long enough chain during its own search; the open question is how long is "enough" — not whether more draws helps once you already have good parameters (that was the wrong question, per the acceptance-rate-fixed-gamma check above returning a rightly-negative answer to a different hypothesis).

**Next:** apply the same $n_{keep}$-vs-optimizer-quality investigation to the single WORST point in the 49-point grid — (delta1=6, delta2=0.2), $\hat L_n=0.0208$, $TS=1296$ (the largest in the entire grid, an extreme outlier even among already-rejected points) — re-fitting at growing $n_{keep}$ levels and tracking $\gamma'g$ variance (and/or acceptance-rate SD) at each fit's own training length, to see where the improvement levels off.

## Worst-point vs. best-point $n_{keep}$ sweep (1000-8000): no monotonic convergence, but a real cross-diagnostic ranking that's robust to $n_{keep}$ — settled on $n_{keep}=3000$, single run/point (2026-09-08/09)

**Full sweep, both (delta1=6,delta2=0.3) ["good"] and (delta1=6,delta2=0.2) ["bad", the worst point in the 49-grid] re-fit independently (same-point-seeded from `lag_m`'s own best fit, no chaining) at $n_{keep}\in\{1000,2000,3000,4000,5000,6000,7000,8000\}$, $n_{burn}=1000$ throughout.** Result: **neither point shows a monotonically improving trajectory on $\gamma'g$ variance** — (6,0.3)'s own variance bounces from 33.60 (1000) up to 121.00 (2000) and 104.16 (3000) before dropping to 10.25 (5000) and 10.65 (8000); (6,0.2) never drops below ~48 anywhere in the range. The earlier "clean 3.3x improvement from 1000→5000" (previous entry) was real for those two specific checkpoints but doesn't describe the full trajectory once the gaps are filled in — the process is noisy throughout for BOTH points.

**But $\hat L_n$ and acceptance-rate SD tell a consistent, robust story across all 8 independent re-optimizations: (6,0.3) beats (6,0.2) at EVERY single $n_{keep}$ level, no exceptions**, on both metrics (avg $\hat L_n$ 0.0058 vs 0.0093; avg acceptance-SD 0.076 vs 0.151, almost exactly half). $\gamma'g$ variance, once the full trajectory is seen (not just two cherry-picked endpoints), is NOT a reliable discriminator on its own — acceptance-rate SD is. **User's key methodological correction, recorded verbatim for future reference:** it is *expected*, not a flaw, that some (delta1,delta2) points never converge to a low-variance regime no matter how long the chain runs — if every point eventually converged given enough draws, the $\chi^2$ test would never reject anything and the confidence region would be vacuous. Non-convergence at a genuinely bad point is the mechanism by which the test has power, not a numerical failure to fix.

**Discovered a real risk of any single fixed $n_{keep}$: at $n_{keep}=4000$ specifically, the two points' $\hat L_n$ are nearly tied** (0.00747 vs 0.00773, ratio 1.03x) — purely an artifact of which chain length was tested, not evidence the points are actually similar (every other tested $n_{keep}$ shows a 1.3x-3.9x gap). Flagged as a reason not to trust a single evaluation at an arbitrary fixed $n_{keep}$ in general — the more robust design (multi-start/multi-length, keep the best) was proposed but **explicitly declined by the user**: accepting the occasional false negative from a single run is fine given "we are already using conservative CIs anyway" and the ranking signal is strong enough in aggregate.

**Decision for the next full-scale run: $n_{burn}=1000$, $n_{keep}=3000$, ONE run per grid point (no repeats), same-point independent seeding throughout (lambda grid included, for methodological consistency — the lambda grid had never been re-run under this session's independent-seeding convention).** Compute budget accepted knowingly, not chased further.

**Launched overnight (2026-09-09, both jobs backgrounded+disowned to survive session closure):**
1. `Code/C-estimator/run_grid_shards.sh` — the 49-point (delta1,delta2) grid, `n_keep=3000`, 8 concurrent single-threaded shards, `mode=deltagrid`, output `Code/Products/1250-deltagrid-lag_m-nkeep3000-shard{0..7}.csv`.
2. `Code/Deconvolution/run-lambdagrid-nkeep3000-sequential.sh` (new script) — the original 16-point lambda grid, re-run with `n_keep=3000` and independent same-point seeding (the R driver's own auto-generated output filename doesn't vary by lambda, so this runs points SEQUENTIALLY, renaming the output after each call, rather than concurrently like the C++ grid) — 4 cores/call, output `Code/Products/1251-lambdagrid-lag_m-nkeep3000-lam<value>.RData`.

**Next session, in order:** (1) check both overnight runs landed cleanly; (2) find the (delta1,delta2) point from the new grid that passes the $\chi^2_9$ test most robustly (lowest $TS$) — use that as the center for a joint 3-D $(\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ grid; (3) run the 3-D grid; (4) move to the counterfactual (Phase 0/1, see `project_counterfactual_status`).

**Process note, added as a standing memory (`feedback_confirm_before_estimation_runs`):** two runs this session were wasted or silently wrong from an unconfirmed assumption about seeding/chaining (a redundant `lag_m` reseed that had to be killed mid-run; the `par_init`-only-seeds-the-first-point no-op for `lag_2_cal_W`). Going forward, restate the exact seeding/chaining design and get explicit confirmation before launching any medium/large estimation run, even when the user has just described the approach in the same message.

## Joint 3D $(\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ grid expanded to 65 points; dynamic work-stealing + chained Nelder-Mead seeding implemented and validated; Phase 0 declared good enough, moving to Phase 1 (2026-09-09/10)

**Two implementation additions to `Code/C-estimator/grid_estimator.cpp`, both requested to actually exercise findings from earlier in the session rather than leave them as one-off validations:**
1. **Atomic-counter work-stealing** in `compute_dvec_omega_A`'s per-firm thread split, replacing the static `chunk = ceil(n/n_threads)` division. Rationale: on Apple Silicon's asymmetric P/E cores, a static split leaves whichever thread lands on an E-core as a straggler the other threads all wait on at `join()`; a shared `std::atomic<int>` index handed out via `fetch_add(1, memory_order_relaxed)` lets every thread keep pulling the next unclaimed firm until none remain, so wall time is bounded by aggregate throughput, not the slowest core's fixed share. Only matters when `n_threads>1` per grid point — the original 27-point run used `n_shards=12, n_threads=1` (one point per core, no internal split at all), so this fix was inert there; the follow-up 38-point run switched to `n_shards=4, n_threads=3` specifically to exercise it.
2. **Chained nearest-neighbor seeding** for `run_grid3d_mode`, replacing "every point seeds from the same fixed x0." New `seed_csv` CLI option loads a reference set (any prior grid3d output, 23-column format) as an initial "solved" pool; a greedy shell-expansion loop repeatedly solves whichever remaining point is closest (in normalized $(\delta_1/0.75,\delta_2/0.10,\log_{10}\lambda/0.33)$ step-unit space) to any already-solved point (reference set, or a point this same shard has itself already solved this run), seeding from that neighbor's converged $(\delta_0,\eta,\gamma)$. No cross-shard coordination — each shard only sees the shared reference set plus its own progress. Smoke-tested against a live 3-point dry run before the real launch.

**Launch: 4 shards $\times$ 3 threads (12 total, matching core count), `algo=neldermead`, `n_burn=1000,n_keep=3000`, `seed_csv`=the existing 27-point results (with the one non-converged point from that batch, $(\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2)=(7\times10^{-7},4.2,0.6)$, excluded from the seed pool so it wouldn't re-seed from its own bad fit) — 37 new points (one-layer outer-cube expansion: $\delta_1\in\{3.5,4.2,5.0,\mathbf{5.8}\}$, $\delta_2\in\{0.4,0.5,0.6,\mathbf{0.7}\}$, $\lambda\in\{\mathbf{1.3\times10^{-7}},3\times10^{-7},7\times10^{-7},1.5\times10^{-6}\}$, bold = new level) plus the one re-run point, 38 total.**

**Result: all 38 converged cleanly (`conv=4`, none hit `maxtime=6000`), averaging 1409s/point (879–2151s range) vs. the prior 27-point run's 4755s/point average — a 3.4x speedup, holding steady across the whole batch, combining both the threading and chaining effects.** The re-run straggler point, previously `MAXTIME_REACHED` under independent 1-thread seeding, converged cleanly this time at $\hat L_n=0.000460$ (passing) — the chained+multithreaded seed fixed the specific point that failed before, not just sped up the rest.

**Substantive finding: the grid's own minimum moved, and $\lambda$ turned out to be the dominant axis, not $\delta_1,\delta_2$.** New global minimum across all 65 points: $\lambda=1.3\times10^{-7}$, $(\delta_1,\delta_2)=(3.5,0.5)$, $\hat L_n=0.000345$ — lower than the original 27-point grid's minimum (0.000405 at $\delta_1=3.5,\delta_2=0.4$), and at the new lowest tested $\lambda$ level. Reading the pass/fail pattern from only the first 27 points (higher $\delta_1,\delta_2$ denser at low-mid $\lambda$) turned out not to extrapolate: once $\lambda=1.3\times10^{-7}$ is reached, passing points span nearly the whole tested $\delta_1,\delta_2$ range, and high $\lambda$ ($1.5\times10^{-6}$) remains uniformly and often badly rejected regardless of $\delta_1,\delta_2$. This mirrors the standalone 16-point $\lambda$-only grid's own finding (no lower turnaround found down to $10^{-14}$) — read as a real structural feature (Colombia's detection probability genuinely low this period), not an unfinished search.

**Conservative (Theorem F.1) absolute test checked at multiple confidence levels on the full 65-point set — still rejects everywhere, but much more narrowly than before:** 0/65 pass at 90%, 95%, or 99% CI; 6/65 pass only at 99.9% (not a standard level). At the new minimum, $TS=22.26$ vs. $\chi^2_{9,.99}=21.67$ — under 5% away in $\hat L_n$ terms, a much closer miss than the original grid's minimum was. Does not change the operative choice (the min-subtracted CHT-style test remains the one used for the confidence set, per the earlier session's decision) but is worth knowing the absolute test isn't failing by an order of magnitude anymore.

**Decision (user's call, 2026-09-10): stop chasing tighter Phase-0 bounds and move to Phase 1 (counterfactual).** The six points closest to clearing even the 99.9% conservative bar were noted as broadly consistent with both the original 27-point grid's minimum and the much earlier (pre-grid-search) coordinate-wise estimates ($\delta_1\approx4.1$–$4.2$) — read as reassurance that the "sensible" region and the "best-fitting" region aren't disconnected, even though the raw minimum sits at $\delta_1=3.5$. Operating point selected for Phase 1: $(\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2)=(1.3\times10^{-7},4.2,0.5)$ (the user's explicit choice, "the middle point," not the raw minimum) — full converged fit and reasoning recorded in CLAUDE.md's "Current stage-2 status" section.

**Artifacts:** `Code/Products/1258-grid3d-newpoints.csv` (new points), `1258-grid3d-seedpool.csv` (filtered seed reference), `1258-grid3d-expand-shard{0..3}.csv`/`.Rout` (raw shard outputs), `1258-grid3d-lag_m-combined-full.csv` (all 65 points combined), `Paper/images/1259-stage2-grid3d-cube-expanded.png` (updated cube plot, same manual `persp()`+`trans3d()` construction as the original 27-point version).

## $\eta$ identified as stealing $\lambda$'s identification and removed; $\lambda$ finally shows a bounded confidence region (2026-09-10)

**Trigger: Phase 1's forward-simulated counterfactual revenue collapsed to deeply negative values almost instantly for $\Delta>0$.** At the (then-current, $\eta$-including) operating point $\lambda=1.3\times10^{-7}$, mean real revenue crossed zero between $\Delta=0.01$ and $\Delta=0.02$ and reached -\$57,568 by $\Delta=0.5$ — traced to the closed-form $e'(\Delta)=\max(0,(\Delta+2\lambda e_i)/(2\lambda(1+\Delta)))$ blowing up because $1/(2\lambda)\approx3.85$ million, dwarfing any real firm's size, so even a modest $\Delta$ implies evasion many multiples of a firm's own materials purchases. This was the first *concrete, numerical* symptom of a problem that had until now only looked like an inconclusive data fact (no $\lambda$-grid ever finding a lower CI bound).

**Root cause, found by checking `draw_from_rho_eta`'s own floor `max(eta*Mstar, Mstar-1/(2*lambda))` directly against the data:** at $\hat\lambda=1.3\times10^{-7}$, **0 of 28,892 interior firms** ever had the $\lambda$-side term bind — $\eta$ alone determined every firm's sampler support. The user's own diagnosis, confirmed and extended in-session: $\eta$ is a single global relative rate, so whenever it binds, *every* such firm gets the identical $e/M=1/\eta-1$ — a second mechanism, entirely capable of substituting for $\lambda$'s own ceiling, meaning the optimizer could push $\hat\lambda\to0$ at zero cost to fit throughout this whole project's history. Two ideas were explored and set aside before landing on the fix: (1) a hit-and-run/hybrid MCMC update for $\lambda$ itself (rejected on reflection — $\lambda$ stays an outer NLopt parameter, "the theta parameter for expected revenue" the user meant was $R$ itself in the counterfactual, unrelated); (2) a static percentile-based lower bound on $\lambda$ from the $M^*$ distribution with explicit firm-dropping (superseded by the cleaner realization that simply removing $\eta$ and letting the *existing* FOC-domain-only support restriction bind naturally reproduces the desired "big firms' evasion gets squeezed, small firms stay free" behavior, continuously, with no dropping).

**Fix, `Code/Rcpp/1200-stage2-elvis-common.h` + `Code/C-estimator/grid_estimator.cpp`:** new `draw_from_rho_checked(rng,Mstar,lambda)` — the pre-$\eta$ `draw_from_rho` analytic construction (`max(0,Mstar-1/(2*lambda))`, no rejection loop needed since the bound is exact) plus a recursive redraw as a numerical safety net when the raw (pre-floor) FOC value falls inside the same `H_DENOM_FLOOR=1e-6` margin already treated as unreliable elsewhere. Verified empirically, not just argued, via a new `redrawdiag` mode: **0 redraws in 115.6M draws** at the actual operating $\lambda$, and **exactly the theoretically-predicted $\approx10^{-6}$ rate** (116/115.6M) at the extreme upper $\lambda$ bound where the ceiling is guaranteed to bind for every firm — the math (`raw_denom` collapses to exactly `1-u01` when the ceiling binds) and the measurement agree to 4 significant figures. $\eta$ dropped from `grid3d`, `deltagrid`, `revenue_baseline`, `revgrid`, and the new `lambdagrid` modes (diagnostic-only modes left on the old convention, out of scope). 

**$\lambda$ bounds:** $\lambda$'s NLopt bounds widened to $[1.6285\times10^{-8},0.016129]$, derived from the $M^*$ distribution's own two extremes (`1/(2*max(Mstar))` = "nothing is evasion," `1/(2*min(Mstar))` = "all is evasion").

**Center refit at the new bounds (single point, $\delta_1=4.2,\delta_2=0.5$ fixed, $\lambda,\delta_0,\gamma$ free):** $\hat\lambda=2.202\times10^{-7}$, $\hat L_n=0.000479$ — same order of magnitude as the old $\eta$-based estimate, not pinned at either new bound, no red flags.

**Coarse 8-point $\lambda$-only grid (free $\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2,\gamma$, same-point independent seeding from the center refit) — first genuine bounded rejection pattern ever seen on this axis.** 7/8 points reject decisively (TS 241–7130 vs. $\chi^2_{9,.95}=16.92$); exactly one passes, $\lambda=1.17\times10^{-7}$ ($\hat L_n=0.00227$, TS$_{\text{soft}}=0$ by construction since it's this grid's own min, TS$_{\text{hard}}=146$ — clears the operative min-subtracted CHT test but not the conservative Theorem F.1 absolute test, consistent with the standing decision on which test to use). $\hat\delta_1,\hat\delta_2>0$ at *every* tested point, not just the best one — the U-shaped-cost sign prediction holds throughout even though the specific $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ values differ noticeably from point to point (implied $\omega^*\approx2.1$–$3.5$ vs. the earlier $\eta$-era $\sim4.0$–$4.2$). **User's own correction, worth keeping verbatim: this shift in $\delta_1,\delta_2$ is not evidence of a broken search — the old $\delta$'s were plausibly biased/distorted because $\lambda$ wasn't doing its share of the identifying work, so $\delta_1,\delta_2$ had to carry it; now that $\lambda$ is doing its own job, of course the other estimates move.** `max|gamma|` mostly single-to-low-double-digits, one outlier at 93.0 (the point adjacent to the passing one) — flagged, not treated as alarming (Schennach: large $|\gamma|$ is an expected degenerate-tilt limit).

**Two-level work-stealing built for `lambdagrid` (`run_lambdagrid_mode`), replacing the shard-script launcher for this mode.** Motivated by watching the coarse grid run live: the old static `idx % n_shards` assignment left two shards finished (both their points done) while two others ground through a single hard point, wasting the finished shards' cores. Fix: one process, a shared atomic counter over grid *points* (not firms) feeding `n_groups=min(n_points,n_threads_total)` concurrent `std::thread` "group leaders," each of which calls the existing single-point fit function with its own share of threads (remainder threads distributed to the first few groups so the full budget is always used exactly) — and each such call already does firm-level work-stealing internally, unchanged. Deliberately NOT special-cased for $n_{\text{points}}=1$: the same formula collapses to 1 group with all threads automatically. Smoke-tested (single point → all threads; 5 points, 8 threads → 5 groups split 1-2 threads each, all complete correctly, no data races) before the real launch.

**Fine 10-point $\lambda$ grid launched (between the two failing neighbors bracketing the passing point, $1.628\times10^{-8}$ to $8.412\times10^{-7}$), running as of this entry.** Next: combine, find the actual soft-CI bounds on both sides of $1.17\times10^{-7}$; then repeat this entire investigation for `lag_2_cal_W` (not yet touched by any of the $\eta$-removal, new-bounds, or new-grid-mode work), starting from the same $\lambda$ range as lag_m per the user's own instruction, seeded from lag_m's fit, widening only if needed.

## Counterfactual revenue collapse traced to a structural property: linear $q$ + linear $\kappa$ makes new evasion an exact linear function of baseline evasion, with a firm-invariant intercept (2026-09-10)

**Motivating question: why does the forward-simulated revenue curve drop so fast after $\Delta>0$?** Re-ran the forward simulation (no optimization) with the new best no-$\eta$ lag_m fit ($\lambda=3.501\times10^{-7}$) — crossing point moved to $\Delta\approx0.033$ (from $\approx0.020$ at $\lambda=2.2\times10^{-7}$ and $\approx0.012$ at the original $\eta$-based $\lambda=1.3\times10^{-7}$), confirming the crossing scales linearly with $\lambda$ to two decimal places across all three fits (ratios 1.70 and 1.59 matching the $\lambda$ ratios 1.69 and 1.59 exactly) — strong evidence this is the model's own math, not noise from any one fit.

**The user's own question sharpened the mechanism precisely: is $e'(\Delta)$ weighted by the firm's original evasion?** Expanding the closed form settles it exactly:
$$e'(\Delta)=\frac{\Delta+2\lambda e_i}{2\lambda(1+\Delta)}=\underbrace{\frac{e_i}{1+\Delta}}_{\text{weighted by }e_i}+\underbrace{\frac{\Delta}{2\lambda(1+\Delta)}}_{\text{same for every firm}}$$
Yes and no: $e_i$ enters with slope $1/(1+\Delta)$, but there's a second, additive term with no $e_i$ (or $\tau_P,\rho_t,\omega,\psi$) dependence at all — identical across the entire firm population. Verified this is exact, not approximate, by re-deriving from the FOC directly: $\kappa$ linear in $e$ $\Rightarrow$ $\partial\kappa/\partial e=C(\omega,\psi)$ is a *constant* (not a function of $e$) $\Rightarrow$ the FOC $\tau\rho_t(1-2\lambda e)=C$ is linear in $e$ $\Rightarrow$ a benefit-side shock ($\Delta$) shifts every firm's equilibrium $e$ by the *same absolute amount*, independent of firm size or current evasion level. This is THE reason the intercept term is firm-invariant, and it's a mechanical consequence of the headline linear-$\kappa$ specification, not a bug in the closed-form derivation — a cost function with increasing marginal cost (e.g. quadratic in $e$) would instead dampen the response for firms already evading heavily.

**User's own framing of the scope caveat, worth keeping verbatim: under this linear assumption, the counterfactual shouldn't be pushed too far in $\Delta$, because every firm's evasion increases by the same uniform shift on top of a term that already embeds their productivity and cost draw — and since $(\omega,\psi)$ are held fixed throughout the exercise (by construction, per the Nail Kashaev correction against "recovering and holding fixed" a type), the model has no way to let firms respond on any OTHER margin (output, materials sourcing, exit) that a large tax change would plausibly trigger in reality.** This is now recorded as a settled structural property of the model (CLAUDE.md's Counterfactual section) rather than a one-off empirical curiosity.

**Standing action item, explicitly requested: add this mechanism (the linear-in-$e_i$ decomposition, the constant-marginal-cost derivation, and the "don't push $\Delta$ too far" scope caveat) to the `600-opt-tax.qmd` slide deck's Counterfactual section**, once slides are next being worked on — not yet done as of this entry.

## Diminishing marginal returns to a purchases-tax cut identify an economically motivated "optimal" stopping point — the headline story (2026-09-10)

**Motivation: having established that revenue rises monotonically as $\Delta\to-1$ with no interior reversal (confirmed by exploratory sims, not the optimization grid — user's own framing: "only sims like we're doing"), the obvious next question is whether that increase is linear, and if not, where the curvature is.** Three pure forward-simulation exercises at the best no-$\eta$ lag_m operating point ($\lambda=3.501\times10^{-7},\hat\delta_1=4.315,\hat\delta_2=0.541$), all deterministic $e_i$-by-$e_i$ resolves off the same fixed draws, no optimization: a fine $\Delta\in[-0.5,0.5]$ grid at step 0.01 (`1275`), an onset sweep $\Delta\in[0,0.02]$ at step 0.001 to find exactly where revenue turns negative (`1276`), and a negative extension $\Delta\in[-0.95,-0.5]$ at step 0.05 to see how far the increase continues (`1277`).

**Answer: revenue is exactly linear (constant first differences) from $\Delta\approx-0.95$ up to $\approx-0.20$, curved between $-0.20$ and the zero-crossing at $\Delta\approx0.0325$ (real terms, interpolated), and collapses beyond.** Checked precisely later the same day (first differences of `1275`'s real revenue column, step 0.01) to pin down exact zone boundaries for a presentation plot: Zone 1 ($\Delta\in[-0.95,-0.20]$) has essentially constant first differences ($-7.4326$ per step, drifting under 0.1% by $-0.20$) — genuinely linear. Zone 2 ($\Delta\in[-0.20,0.0325]$) is the only zone with real, sharp curvature — first differences accelerate from $-7.44$ to $-59.7$ right at $\Delta=0$, then jump to $\approx-520$ just past the crossing — this is where the diminishing-returns argument and the $\Delta\approx-0.20/-0.25$ optimum both live. Zone 3 ($\Delta>0.0325$) is NOT exactly linear either on close inspection (first differences drift smoothly from $-514.5$ at $0.04$ down to $-387.2$ at $0.50$, a real quadratic-ish curve) — it just looks flat/linear next to zone 2's near-vertical transition. So "two linear zones and one nonlinear" is a fair description at presentation resolution, with zone 2 the one worth zooming a plot into.

**Built the zoomed zone-2-only plot (`Code/Deconvolution/1279-simulated-laffer-headline-plot.R` → `Paper/images/1279-simulated-laffer-headline.png`) and caught a scaling mistake before it shipped.** First pass computed the MR panel using raw first differences (not divided by the 0.01 step) and separately assumed, without checking, that the interesting range topped out around ~$520 — both wrong. Recomputing properly (same $MR(\Delta)=[R(\Delta-0.01)-R(\Delta)]/0.01$ definition as the headline finding) showed the true range inside zone 2 alone is $745$ to $52{,}932$ — nearly 2 orders of magnitude — because there's a SECOND, sharper kink sitting exactly at $\Delta=0$: $MR$ rises smoothly from $745$ (near $-0.20$) to $5{,}970$ right at $\Delta=0$, then jumps a further $\approx9\times$ to $52{,}932$ in the very next 1% step. A linear y-axis (or a wrong assumed range) would have hidden the $745\to1{,}359$ diminishing-returns story entirely underneath this much bigger jump; fixed with a log-scale MR panel so both features — the gentle acceleration through most of the zone and the sharp kink right at current policy — are visible at once.

**Revised again after the user pushed back on the log-scale version: "are we mixing marginal revenue increases and decreases?"** Worked out the actual mechanism precisely rather than just re-styling the plot. Every firm's own unclip threshold is $\Delta=-2\lambda e_i$ (always $\le0$); with $\lambda=3.501\times10^{-7}$, the median firm ($e_i\approx841$) has threshold $\Delta\approx-0.00059$ — essentially at zero. The intercept term's slope at $\Delta=0$ is $1/(2\lambda)\approx1{,}428{,}163$, shared by every unclipped firm. So a large mass of firms (all with small-to-median $e_i$) cross their own threshold and "switch on" this huge slope almost simultaneously in a very narrow window just below $\Delta=0$ — a real, structural pile-up (confirmed against `1276`'s 0.001-step onset grid: $MR\approx53{,}165$ already at $\Delta=0.001$, not a gradual ramp), not a sign-mixing bug. But once $\Delta>0$ every firm is unclipped (thresholds always $\le0$), a genuinely different regime from $\Delta<0$ where most firms are still clipped — so the right fix wasn't better log-scale styling, it was splitting the MR panel into two separately-scaled linear panels ("cutting further" vs. "past current policy"), since these answer different policy questions and conflating them on one continuum (even correctly, via log scale) invites exactly the "are you mixing something up" reaction the user had.

**Caught a real bug while rebuilding: `1275`'s $\Delta=0$ row is stored as `1.04083e-17` (float noise from grid generation), and my exact `Delta <= 0` filter silently dropped it** — the left ("cutting further") panel was rendering without its own rightmost, most important point ($MR=5{,}970$ at $\Delta=0$) as a result, understating the story right where it mattered most. Fixed by rounding `Delta` to 6 decimals immediately after reading this file, before any filter.

**A THIRD revision, same day, after the split-panel version still confused the user: "increasing a little after zero increased marginal revenue a lot! that cannot be... where is that number coming from... does not make sense."** Traced the exact number: $R(\Delta=0)=1{,}700.92$, $R(\Delta=0.001)=1{,}647.76$ — a raw revenue LOSS of $\$53.17$ over that first 0.1pp step (direction correct: revenue falls as $\Delta$ rises past zero, consistent with the collapse, not a contradiction of it). The genuinely misleading part was dividing that $\$53.17$ by the step size ($0.001$) to express it "per unit $\Delta$" (the same convention validly used on the cut side, where the slope really is close to constant over a *wide* range, $-0.95$ to $-0.20$) — on the increase side the slope only holds locally over a fraction of a percentage point (already down 2% by $\Delta=0.02$), so extrapolating a tiny, genuinely small step to "per unit $\Delta$" manufactures a huge, not-actually-meaningful number ($53{,}165$). **Fix: relabel and recompute the right panel as raw $\$$ lost per the ACTUAL 0.1pp step shown (not rescaled), and add a matched-step-size comparison line** — zone 1's own last step, rescaled DOWN to the same 0.1pp size, is $\$5.97$; the honest, apples-to-apples jump right at $\Delta=0$ is $\$53/\$5.97\approx8.9\times$, not the "$5{,}970\to52{,}932$" figure from comparing two different step sizes' per-unit-$\Delta$ extrapolations. The underlying mechanism (median firm's threshold $\approx-0.00059$, essentially at $\Delta=0$; shared intercept slope $1/(2\lambda)\approx1.43$M once unclipped) is unchanged and correct — only the *reporting units* on the increase side needed fixing. Final version: `Code/Deconvolution/1279-simulated-laffer-headline-plot.R` → `Paper/images/1279-simulated-laffer-headline.png`.

No reversal anywhere tested down to $-0.95$ — confirms the corner $\tau_P=0$ ($\Delta=-1$) is the *unconstrained* revenue-maximizer of this closed form, exactly as the flat-detection-probability mechanical story predicts (at $\tau_P=0$ there's nothing left to evade, government keeps 100% of $t1$).

**The economically interesting object is the marginal revenue of the next 0.01 of cut, $MR(\Delta)\equiv[R^{\text{real}}(\Delta-0.01)-R^{\text{real}}(\Delta)]/0.01$, not the level.** Computed off `1275`/`1276`'s real (deflated) mean-$R$-per-firm-period column: $MR=1359$ at $\Delta=-0.01$, $866$ at $\Delta=-0.03$, $750$ at $\Delta=-0.10$ — large and falling fast near $\Delta=0$, exactly the region where firms are progressively "unclipping" from the $e'=0$ corner (each firm's own clip threshold is $\Delta=-2\lambda e_i$, tiny given how small $\lambda$ is) and resuming evasion, which is the entire behavioral channel this exercise is trying to capture. By $\Delta\approx-0.20$, $MR$ has flattened to a constant $\approx743$ and **stays exactly there** all the way out to the $-0.90\to-0.95$ leg (step-0.05 first differences: 743.7 at $-0.20$/$-0.21$, 743.2 at $-0.25$/$-0.26$, 743.3 at $-0.40$/$-0.41$, 741.6 at $-0.90$/$-0.95$) — once virtually every firm has hit its own clip, the only thing left driving revenue up is the mechanical $-\text{mean}(\tau_P M)$ term; there is no more behavioral response to buy.

**The headline argument, the user's own framing, confirmed verbatim ("you read my mind, this is the headline story!"): at the optimum, the next marginal cut should buy less than the previous one did — and that's exactly the shape found.** $\Delta\approx-0.20$ to $-0.25$ is the smallest cut that has already captured (essentially) the full behavioral/de-evasion benefit — pushing further to the corner ($\Delta=-1$, $\tau_P=0$) buys the *identical* flat $MR\approx743$ per additional unit of cut, for a vastly larger and economically nonsensical cut (the corner was already flagged, independently, as inconsistent with the cascading-taxation rationale for a purchases-side credit, and as outside the comparative-statics scope caveat above, which holds $(\omega,\psi)$ fixed). This gives a real, diminishing-returns-based answer to "how far should the government cut the purchases tax," distinct from and more defensible than the model's own unconstrained corner — the intended headline result of Phase 1.

**Final headline plot settled after several rounds of iteration on HOW to visualize it, 2026-09-10: `Code/Deconvolution/1280-pct-change-from-prev-plot.R` → `Paper/images/1280-pct-change-from-prev.png`.** The path there: raw-dollar MR (misread scale), elasticity-vs-base (clean but hid the step-to-step story), elasticity-from-previous-step at fine 0.001 resolution on both sides (technically fine, but the user explicitly preferred the coarser framing: "I liked it better before... a 1% cut buys 3%, that's the headline"). **Settled design: deliberately MIXED step size** — cut side at its native 1pp resolution (`1275`), increase side at its native 0.1pp resolution (`1276`) — because the asymmetry in step size *is* the point: a full 1% cut is needed to buy +3.51%, while a mere 0.1% increase (a tenth the move) already costs -3.13%, nearly erasing that entire gain. Y-axis is %-change-from-the-previous-step (not vs. base), Δ=0 left as a genuine gap (nothing plotted there, not even an interpolated point) per the user's explicit instruction that zero is the base and there's nothing to report there. Three annotated callouts carry the exact headline numbers directly on the chart: "1st 1% cut: +3.51%," "next 1% cut: only +0.77%," "a mere 0.1% increase: -3.13%."

**Byproduct, kept for potential future use but NOT part of the final headline plot: `Code/Products/1281-revenue-negfine-lag_m.csv`** — a new fine (0.001-step) forward-simulated revenue grid for $\Delta\in[-0.04,-0.001]$, generated via direct `revenue_baseline` CLI calls (looped in bash, ~0.4s/point) at the exact same operating point/par vector as `1275`/`1276`/`1277` (verified to reproduce `1275`'s $-0.02$ and $-0.04$ rows exactly bit-for-bit). Built when the user asked for finer resolution left of zero; ultimately not used in the final plot once the user preferred the coarser 1pp framing for the headline message specifically — but it's a validated asset if finer cut-side resolution is wanted elsewhere (e.g. the eventual `revgrid` test-inversion work).

## The real (Δ,R) test-inversion grid: planned, a new seeding mode built, and launched overnight (2026-09-10)

**With the headline pure-simulation story settled, moved to the actual inference question: does a real confidence set support it?** Planned the grid in chat before touching any code, per the standing convention. Key design choices, all the user's own calls:

1. **Seeding: one anchor cell, not per-R-column chaining.** The existing `revgrid` mode chains $\gamma$ sequentially outward from each R-shard's own independently-refit $\Delta=0$ point — a different scheme from what the user wanted. Instead: solve ONE cell externally first ($\Delta=0$, $R=\$1{,}700.92$ — the pure-sim baseline), with $\gamma_{1..9}$ seeded from lag_m's best fit and $\gamma_{10}$ (the new revenue moment's own multiplier — no prior estimate exists for it) started from an arbitrary small guess ($10^{-5}$, chosen by analogy to how other small-magnitude moment rows' gammas, e.g. $\gamma_7,\gamma_8\sim10^{-6}$–$10^{-10}$, need very small multipliers to keep $\gamma\cdot g$ inside a sane exponent range). Then broadcast that ONE converged $(\delta_0,\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2,\gamma_{1..10})$ as the identical seed for every other cell — same-point independent seeding, no further chaining — directly reapplying the lesson already validated for `lambdagrid` ("seed every point independently from the SAME already-validated point, don't chain sequentially").
2. **$\Delta$ grid retargeted to the zone of interest actually mapped this session**, not the old generic $\{-0.5,-0.25,0,0.5,0.75\}$ spread: $\{-0.04,-0.03,-0.02,-0.01,0,0.001,0.01\}$ — exactly the range and resolution of the finalized headline-numbers plot (`1280`).
3. **$R$ grid**: centered on each $\Delta$'s own pure-forward-simulation baseline (already known precisely from `1275`/`1276`), $\times\{0.8,0.9,1.0,1.1,1.2\}$ — 5 points per $\Delta$, 35 cells total. Chosen over a generic uniform spread since we now have accurate baselines to center on, unlike the original (pre-$\eta$-removal) design.
4. **Bounds**: checked the code before proposing any change — $\lambda\in[1.6285\times10^{-8},0.016129]$ (`REVGRID_LAMBDA_LO/HI`) already exactly matches the lambdagrid bounds; $\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2$ already bounded at $\pm60$ (`DELTA_BOUND`, effectively free given fits are all under 5); $\gamma$'s already unbounded. No bound changes needed at all.

**Code change: new `revgrid_indep` mode, added alongside (not replacing) the existing `revgrid`.** `run_revenue_grid_indep_mode` in `Code/C-estimator/grid_estimator.cpp` flattens the $(\Delta,R)$ grid into one list and reuses `run_lambdagrid_mode`'s exact two-level work-stealing pattern (atomic counter over cells feeding `n_groups=min(n_cells,n_threads)` concurrent thread-groups, each with its own firm-level work-stealing) — valid here specifically because removing the chaining also removes the only reason cells needed a particular processing order. Compiled clean (only the pre-existing, unrelated Accelerate-framework deprecation warnings). Smoke-tested with a tiny 2×2 grid (30s maxtime, `n_burn=50/n_keep=200`) before trusting it — confirmed correct: all 4 cells seeded identically (no chaining), work-stealing split 8 threads across 4 groups correctly, output columns line up as expected. Separately verified the CSV-column-parsing step the orchestration script depends on (`cut -d',' -f3-16` on a `revgrid_indep` output row extracts exactly `delta0_hat,lambda_hat,delta1_hat,delta2_hat,gamma1..10` in the right order to feed back in as `x0`) against the actual smoke-test output before trusting it in the unattended pipeline.

**One practical limitation discovered while building the orchestration script: the CLI's R-grid is shared across ALL Deltas in a single `revgrid_indep` call (full cross-product), but this design wants a DIFFERENT R-grid per Delta.** Rather than extend the C++ parsing to support per-Delta R-lists (more risk, less time before the user needed to leave for the day), worked around it at the shell level: `Code/Deconvolution/run-revgrid-indep-lag_m.sh` runs 7 separate `revgrid_indep` calls (one per $\Delta$, each with its own 5-value R-grid), all sharing the identical anchor-derived `x0`, then concatenates the 8 output files (anchor + 7 batches) into one combined CSV. Each batch still gets full two-level work-stealing across its own 5 cells and all 12 threads.

**Launched detached (`nohup`+`disown`, survives session/shell closure) at 23:44, 2026-09-10.** `n_burn=1000,n_keep=3000`, `maxtime=1800`s per NM pass (2 passes/cell, so 60 min/cell worst case) as a safety cap — chosen from `lambdagrid`'s observed per-cell range (7–115 min for a similarly-sized 13–14 parameter problem). Expected total: roughly 3–6 hours (anchor + 7 batches, each batch's wall time bounded by its slowest of 5 cells run at ~2–3 threads/cell via work-stealing), comfortably inside an overnight window; a background wait-loop was also set up to report back on completion, independent of the actual computation (which does not depend on that wait-loop surviving). Outputs: `Code/Products/1282-revgrid-anchor-lag_m.csv` (Stage 1), `1282-revgrid-lag_m-delta{m04,m03,m02,m01,0,p001,p01}.csv` (Stage 2, 7 files), `1282-revgrid-lag_m-combined.csv` (final, all 8 concatenated), log at `1282-revgrid-lag_m-launch.log`.

**Not yet done, for next session:** check the combined output landed cleanly (all 35 cells + anchor converged, no crashes); compute $TS=2n\hat L_n$ per cell against $\chi^2_{10,.95}$ (note $d_g=10$ here, not 9 — the auxiliary $R$ moment adds one row); build the test-inversion confidence-set figure (min/max of passing $(\Delta,R)$ pairs, AK2020-style, per the standing Inference plan in CLAUDE.md); compare the resulting CI against the pure-simulation headline numbers to see whether the point estimates survive real inference.

## Overnight run landed vacuous (every cell passes); traced to $\lambda$ being left free at each cell; literature check confirms AK2020 fixes $\theta$ in their own counterfactual, Schennach never addresses the question (2026-09-11)

**The overnight `revgrid_indep` run (36 cells) finished cleanly in ~4h34min** — all converged (mostly `conv=4`/`XTOL_REACHED`, one `conv=6`/`MAXTIME_REACHED`), and $\hat\delta_1\approx4.339,\hat\delta_2\approx0.553$ stayed essentially identical across every single cell — the cleanest, most stable run of this whole project (no basin-wandering at all, unlike every earlier $\lambda$-only or $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ grid).

**But the test result itself was vacuous: every one of 36 cells passes, both the soft and hard tests, by a huge margin.** $TS=2n\hat L_n$ topped out at $0.448$ against $\chi^2_{10,.95}=18.31$ (and $\chi^2_{10,.99}=23.21$, checked explicitly when the user asked "what if we take the hard test" — trivially still all-pass, since a larger critical value can only make passing easier, not harder; there is no test-statistic-threshold combination under which any of these 36 cells would reject, given how small every $\hat L_n$ already is). This means the tested R-grid ($\pm20\%$ of each $\Delta$'s own pure-sim baseline) was far too narrow to find where rejection starts — not a finding, a design problem, confirmed by fitting a quadratic to $TS$ vs. \%-deviation at each $\Delta$ and extrapolating: 6 of 7 legs pointed to needing roughly $\pm130$–$150\%$ deviation before crossing the threshold, and the 7th ($\Delta=+1\%$) is dominated by pure noise at this deviation range (raw $\hat L_n$ values of $9.9\times10^{-12}$ to $1.5\times10^{-10}$ show no discernible U-shape at all — can't extrapolate that leg yet).

**Before relaunching with a wider R-grid, the user asked the sharper question: why is $\theta_{\text{smooth}}=(\lambda,\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ being reprofiled at every cell in the first place, rather than fixed?** The economic argument, laid out directly: we already know $\lambda$ is a *weakly-identified-once-it's-free* parameter (that's the entire $\eta$-removal story from the day before) — if $\lambda$ is left free at every $(\Delta,R)$ cell, it can silently readjust the implied detection probability to make almost *any* candidate $R$ look consistent with the data, exactly the mechanism that made $\eta$ dangerous, one level up. **Checked directly against the just-finished run's own output: confirmed, dramatically.** $\hat\lambda$ ranges $1.63\times10^{-8}$ to $9.19\times10^{-7}$ across the 35 cells — a $56\times$ spread — and swings $43\times$ *within a single* $\Delta=-4\%$ across just its own 5 $R$ candidates ($1.69\times10^{-8}$ to $7.28\times10^{-7}$). The "same-point independent seeding, no chaining" fix from the day before only prevented cross-cell *drift* into different basins (which it did — $\delta_1,\delta_2$ stayed rock-stable) — it never stopped $\lambda$ from moving *within* each cell's own optimization to whatever value best rationalizes that cell's candidate $R$. Seeding and fixing are different things; this run only did the former.

**Literature check, performed before adopting the fix (not just asserted): does AK2020 or Schennach say anything about fixing vs. profiling structural parameters when constructing a policy counterfactual?** Full-text search of `ELVIS.pdf` and `ELVIS_supplement.pdf`: zero occurrences of "counterfactual." Schennach's 2022 JEL survey: exactly one occurrence, a throwaway motivating clause in the introduction with no methodology attached. She simply never addresses this question. AK2020's own counterfactual application (`Lit-Papers/ReplicationAK2020/Appendix_F/`) is the only real precedent, checked both in the paper text and the actual Julia code: `F_main_shell.jl` hardcodes `theta0=0.975` as a constant for the entire exercise (their structural/support parameter — the direct analog of our $\lambda$, since both are sampler-support-restriction parameters, not smoothly-profiled GMM nuisance parameters), and the paper's own footnote 67 confirms this in words ("we set... the support of the random discount factor to..."), plus a robustness check that just re-fixes it to a *different* constant rather than re-estimating it. Only `gamma` (their Lagrange multiplier, the full vector, `dg=5`) is ever passed to their optimizers (`bboptimize` then `NLopt.optimize`) in the counterfactual step — `theta0` never enters either. **This directly contradicts a citation already sitting in our own CLAUDE.md**, which cited "AK2020's Appendix F practice" as supporting evidence for profiling nuisance parameters jointly with the counterfactual grid — backwards; corrected the same session (see the ELVIS-conventions bullet). Schennach's Example 1.5/Fig S.3 (the OTHER citation on that same CLAUDE.md bullet) turned out on inspection to be a joint CI for a model's own two full structural parameters, with no separate nuisance block at all — it never spoke to this question either way; the citation there was over-generalized, not wrong per se.
