**Settled design going forward, user's own framing worth keeping verbatim: the counterfactual question is "given these sensible, fixed parameter estimates, what would the counterfactual revenue be, using the distribution of unobservables that is consistent with the data and the most reasonable parameter estimates?" — $\gamma$ freely chosen by the optimizer at each cell IS the "distribution of unobservables" half of that sentence.** $\theta_{\text{smooth}}$ gets fixed at a well-justified point estimate; $\gamma$ (all of it) stays the only thing NLopt touches per cell.

## Slides updated for Tim (committee, not main supervisor, not an ELVIS specialist) — eta cut entirely, moment B dropped, E[e]/Med[e] ballpark recomputed (2026-09-11)

**Context: `650-stage2-prelim-results.qmd` was shown to Nail only, in its old $\eta$-era form; the user is now presenting to Tim and wants the deck brought current, but scrubbed of internal-debugging detail Tim doesn't need.** Explicit instructions: cut everything related to $\eta$ (not just update the numbers — Tim never saw the $\eta$-based version at all, so there's no story to explain); recompute the $E[e]/\text{Med}[e]$ auxiliary check at the new operating points, but flag it plainly as a ballpark forward-simulation reading, not Schennach's formal auxiliary-moment device; drop the entire Moment Set B section (never rerun under the no-$\eta$ mechanism, nothing current to show); on the $\tilde{\mathcal W}_{it-2}$ table row, report the "confidence region" as the single center point (no real interval exists there); add a plain-language note about that instrument's floor-adjacent pass — described to Tim simply as "another point we can't reject, but this instrument is weaker overall," not the mechanistic pile-up explanation used internally; and make the two-instrument $\hat L_n(\lambda)$ comparison plot share one x-axis range, dropping lag_m's higher already-rejected $\lambda$ values that lag_2_cal_W's own grid never covered.

**New code needed for the $E[e]$ ballpark: `grid_estimator.cpp`'s `firm_revenue_baseline_A`/`run_revenue_baseline_mode` never output a per-firm evasion level, only revenue.** Added `e_mean` to `RevenueResult` and accumulated it in the same already-running per-firm Metropolis chain (`e_sum += e_i / n_keep` alongside the existing `R_sum` accumulation) — an additive change, no existing behavior touched. `run_revenue_baseline_mode` now also computes and prints the population mean/median of each firm's own chain-mean $e$, and writes it as a 5th column in the optional per-firm CSV dump. Compiled clean, smoke-tested at `n_burn=50/n_keep=200` before running for real at `n_burn=1000/n_keep=3000` for both instruments' current best-fit points.

**Ballpark results:** $m^*_{it-1}$: $E[e]=2{,}545$, Med$[e]=625$, implied detection probabilities $0.089\%$/$0.022\%$. $\tilde{\mathcal W}_{it-2}$: $E[e]=3{,}189$, Med$[e]=685$, $0.173\%$/$0.037\%$. Both right-skewed (mean well above median, a long right tail of aggressive evaders), both comfortably under the 50% FOC ceiling for either instrument.

**New jargon-free comparison plot:** `Code/Deconvolution/1285-lambdagrid-slide-plot.R` → `Paper/images/1285-lambdagrid-slide.png` — same two-instrument $\hat L_n(\lambda)$ comparison as `1278`, but "Not rejected"/"Rejected" instead of test-statistic labels, "detection intensity"/"fit quality" instead of $\lambda$/$\hat L_n$ jargon, and lag_m's grid filtered to $\lambda\le8.412\times10^{-7}$ so both panels share the same x-axis window instead of lag_m's own extra (already-decisively-rejected) higher-$\lambda$ tail stretching its own axis out further than lag_2_cal_W's.

**Also appended to `600-opt-tax.qmd`'s Counterfactual section** (after its existing MSL-era numerical strategy, before the commented-out MSM alternative): a "Headline Result" / "Why" / "The Optimal Cut, Not the Corner" sequence covering the diminishing-returns finding and the linear-$e_i$-decomposition mechanism — the standing reminder tracked in memory since the finding first landed, now resolved.

**Next: a stronger point estimate to fix $\theta$ at.** The 1-D $\lambda$-only lambdagrid (holding $\delta_1,\delta_2$ merely "free," not jointly gridded) is one argument for $\hat\theta$, but the user wants a second, stronger one: rerun the joint 3D $(\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ grid — the one that produced the original 65-point Phase-0 cube — under the current no-$\eta$ mechanism (that cube predates the $\eta$-removal fix entirely and is superseded for this purpose). A genuinely jointly-bounded passing region in all three dimensions at once is the stronger argument the user is after. Planning this cube is the immediate next task.

## Diagnostic deltagrid before the cube: confirms the passing region is tight, finds a joint optimum BETTER than the lambdagrid's own, and settles the cube's spacing after a real disagreement (2026-09-11)

**Before committing to cube spacing, ran a 25-point diagnostic first: `deltagrid` mode (moment set A, $\lambda$ free, $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ on a $5\times5$ grid, $\pm\{1,2\}$ in $\delta_1$ and $\pm\{0.2,0.4\}$ in $\delta_2$ around the lambdagrid's own best fit), same-point independent seeding (already the mode's own default design, no code change needed), NM, `n_burn=1000/n_keep=3000`.** Smoke-tested (3 points, 20s budget) before the real launch; ran via the existing `run_grid_shards.sh` (4 shards × 3 threads), ~3h5min wall time.

**Result: only two of 25 cells pass** ($\chi^2_{9,.95}=16.9$) — the minimum at $(\delta_1,\delta_2)=(4.3,0.54)$ (TS=0 by construction) and a second, weaker point at $(5.3,0.74)$ (TS=2.5) — and critically, **every immediate cross-neighbor of the minimum (one step away in either $\delta_1$ or $\delta_2$ alone) already rejects decisively**, TS ranging 20 to 180. Confirmed visually two ways: a heatmap (`Code/Deconvolution/1286-deltagrid-heatmap-plot.R` → `Paper/images/1286-deltagrid-heatmap-lag_m.png`) and a `persp()` 3D surface with $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ as the base plane and $\hat L_n$ as height, matching the exact construction of an earlier eta-era plot (`Code/Deconvolution/1287-deltagrid-lag_m-3dplot.R` → `Paper/images/1287-deltagrid-lag_m-3d.png`) — a visibly jagged, sharp-walled basin, not a smooth bowl.

**Genuinely good news buried in this: the deltagrid's own minimum landed almost exactly on the lambdagrid's best fit** ($\delta_1,\delta_2)=(4.3,0.54)$ vs. $(4.315,0.541)$ — independent cross-validation from an orthogonal profiling direction (one grid frees $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ at fixed $\lambda$; the other frees $\lambda$ at fixed $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$; they agree on where the optimum sits on the $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ plane). But the $\lambda$ each one finds there differs: the lambdagrid's own best fit is $\lambda=3.501\times10^{-7}$ ($\hat L_n=0.0003866$); the deltagrid, at the SAME $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$, finds $\lambda=6.714\times10^{-7}$ fits *better* ($\hat L_n=0.0003705$, about 4% lower). Neither 2D slice has actually solved the joint 3-parameter problem — each only ever profiles two of the three dimensions at a time, which is exactly what a real 3D grid is for. $\hat\lambda=6.714\times10^{-7}$ sits only 24% above the lambdagrid's own upper soft-CI bound ($5.427\times10^{-7}$, just 0.09 of a log10-decade beyond it, against the CI's own 0.38-decade width) — close, not a different regime. The second passing point's $\hat\omega^*=\hat\delta_1/2\hat\delta_2=3.58$ is also close to the center's $3.99$, suggesting (not yet confirming — the diagonal cells between them were never tested) one connected basin rather than two.

**Real disagreement, resolved, over cube spacing.** Proposed finer $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ spacing ($\pm0.5/\pm0.1$ instead of $\pm1/\pm0.2$) for the actual 3D cube, reasoning that the deltagrid's own sharp rejection pattern meant the coarser spacing would likely reject almost everywhere in the cube too, wasting compute without resolving the boundary. **User's correction, worth keeping verbatim: the point of this grid is narrowly evidentiary — justify the specific $\hat\theta$ chosen for the counterfactual, not trace the confidence region's precise shape.** A finer grid would very likely just find a marginally-better point at meaningfully higher cost, without changing the actual decision (which $\hat\theta$ to fix). Sharp, decisive rejection everywhere except a tight, isolated center *is* the wanted result — it's the evidence itself, not a shortcoming of the search. Kept $\pm1/\pm0.2$.

**Cube launched, same day, 27 cells, `grid3d` mode (existing, unchanged).** $\lambda\in\{5.427\times10^{-7},6.714\times10^{-7},8.412\times10^{-7}\}$ — chosen so two of the three slices reuse already-validated lambdagrid boundary points (the CI's own upper edge, and the next point already known to reject past it) rather than guessing a new grid blind; only the center ($6.714\times10^{-7}$, the deltagrid's own joint optimum) is genuinely new. $\delta_1\in\{3.3,4.3,5.3\}$, $\delta_2\in\{0.34,0.54,0.74\}$, same as the deltagrid. Smoke-tested (3 cells, 20s budget, confirmed chained seeding: first point from `x0`, rest from nearest solved neighbor) before the real launch via `run_grid_shards.sh` (4 shards × 3 threads). Data: `Code/Products/1288-cube-lag_m-shard{0..3}.csv`.

**Result, landed same evening: a new best joint operating point, and the first hard-test passes anywhere in the whole project.** New global minimum $\lambda=5.427\times10^{-7}$, $(\hat\delta_1,\hat\delta_2)=(4.3,0.54)$, $\hat\delta_0=3.464$, $\hat L_n=0.0002150$ — lower than either 2D slice ($0.0003866$ lambdagrid, $0.0003705$ deltagrid); neither had actually found the joint optimum, exactly as expected once a genuine 3-parameter grid was run. 8/27 cells pass the soft test: the $(4.3,0.54)$ column passes at all three tested $\lambda$'s (the most robust point by far), while $(3.3,0.34)$ and $(5.3,0.74)$ each pass at two of three with opposite $\lambda$ preferences — consistent with the ridge structure the coarse grid was never meant to resolve in detail, only to confirm exists and stays bounded. **4/27 cells also clear the hard (conservative, Theorem F.1) test** — the new minimum (TS$_{\text{hard}}=13.9$) plus three others at $\lambda=6.714\times10^{-7}$ (TS$_{\text{hard}}=17.0,17.5,20.9$) — a first: every earlier grid, at any trim level, any instrument, any point, failed this test everywhere.

**Visualization request, twice refined in chat.** First pass reused the exact 1259-style construction (axes $\delta_1,\delta_2,\log_{10}\lambda$; ball size $\propto\hat L_n$, bigger=worse). User asked for size to instead mean "bigger=more significant" and asked directly what to use — raw TS would have been backwards (bigger TS = worse fit), so switched to the $p$-value ($1-F_{\chi^2_9}(TS_{\text{soft}})$), bounded in $[0,1]$ and directly interpretable. Second pass: user asked whether any cells clear the hard test (yes, 4) and to mark the global minimum in a distinct color. Final version (`Code/Deconvolution/1289-cube-3dplot.R` → `Paper/images/1289-cube-lag_m-3d.png`): fill = magenta for the single global min / red for other soft passes / blue for rejects; a thick gold outline ring added for anything that also clears the hard test; ball size still $\propto$ p-value.

**This is now the strongest candidate operating point for $\hat\theta$ going into the counterfactual: $(\lambda,\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2)=(5.427\times10^{-7},3.464,4.3,0.54)$**, superseding the lambdagrid's own $(3.501\times10^{-7},3.459,4.315,0.541)$ used for the headline diminishing-returns numbers so far.

## Why $\lambda$ is structurally harder to identify than $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ — a theoretical derivation, not just an empirical pattern (2026-09-11/12)

**Motivated by staring at the cube's own $\delta_0$ column: $\delta_0$ tracks $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ almost mechanically (nearly constant across different $\lambda$'s within a column, jumping when $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ change), while $\hat\omega^*=\delta_1/2\delta_2$ stays remarkably close to $\approx4$ across nearly every point that passes, at every grid this project has run.** First framed the question as an $E[\psi|\omega]$ conditional-mean argument: since $\psi=h(e)-\delta_0+\delta_1\omega-\delta_2\omega^2$ holds firm-by-firm as an identity (the FOC rearranged), and $\psi\perp\omega$ is a maintained model assumption, $E[\psi|\omega]=0 \Rightarrow E[h(e)|\omega]=\delta_0-\delta_1\omega+\delta_2\omega^2$ — a quadratic regression whose coefficients are, in the idealized nonparametric-conditional-mean sense, exactly identified given $\lambda$ (standard polynomial-regression identification, needing only that $\{1,\omega,\omega^2\}$ isn't collinear over $\omega$'s support). Initial read: "identified given $\lambda$, with $\lambda$'s own identification coming from a separate, thinner channel" — presented as basically fine.

**User's direct pushback, and it's the sharper, correct version of the point: for a GIVEN $\lambda$, can't you always just refit $\delta_0$ (and the rest) to match, with $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ unaffected since they capture curvature, not level?** Re-derived properly rather than defending the first answer. The key fact the first pass glossed over: $\psi$ is **linear** in $(\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ for *any* fixed $\lambda$ — $\lambda$ only ever enters through $h(e)$, never through the $\theta_{\text{smooth}}$ coefficients themselves. Moment set A's four $\psi$-only rows ($\psi$, $\psi\omega$, $\psi\omega^2$, $\psi\ln M$) are consequently four *linear* equations in the three unknowns $(\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2)$, holding for whatever $\lambda$ is plugged in. Taking the first three of these: a $3\times3$ linear system, solvable exactly for $(\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ given *any* $\lambda$ at all (generic nonsingularity of $\omega$'s moment matrix is the only requirement). **These three moments therefore have *zero* power to discriminate between candidate $\lambda$'s — not weak power, none, by construction** — confirming the user's intuition exactly and precisely, not just approximately.

**Where $\lambda$'s real identifying power comes from, once this is accounted for:** (1) the 4th $\psi$-moment ($\psi\ln M$) is the only overidentifying restriction within this block, capped by how independently $\ln M$ varies from $\{1,\omega,\omega^2\}$ — likely thin, since $\omega$ is itself built from $\ln M$ via the production-function/AR(1) machinery; (2) the genuinely nonlinear channel, $\ln(1-2\lambda e)$'s curvature, exploited *only* by the $h'_{\text{bounded}}(e,\lambda)\cdot\varepsilon$ score moment — informative only for firms near the FOC ceiling, a thin tail of aggressive evaders. **This reframes the entire session's $\lambda$-identification saga as one consistent structural fact, not a series of unrelated problems**: $\eta$ stealing $\lambda$'s work (because $\eta$ gave the sampler support an alternative, easier way to satisfy the moments without $\lambda$ doing anything), the coarse-vs-fine grid basin instability, and now the $\lambda$-$(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ ridge in the cube — all downstream of $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ being pinned down by a strong, whole-sample linear signal while $\lambda$ only ever gets a thin, nonlinear, tail-only one.

**Confirmed by the user as consistent with prior project history: this is exactly why adding the $h'_{\text{bounded}}\cdot\varepsilon$ score moment (2026-09-07) helped in the first place** — it was, and remains, the *only* source of genuine nonlinear leverage on $\lambda$ in the whole moment set. **Proposed next step, not yet implemented: a second, higher-order score-type moment** (e.g. $E[\varepsilon^2\cdot h'_{\text{bounded}}]$, or another nonlinear-in-$e$ functional) to give $\lambda$ a second, independent nonlinear anchor rather than relying on the single existing score row alone. Flagged for discussion with Nail Kashaev before implementing — a real methodology question, not just an engineering one.

## Fixed-$\theta$ revgrid relaunched overnight — new mode built and validated, but the vacuous result survives; widened R-grid launched autonomously as the planned follow-up (2026-09-12)

**New `revgrid_fixedtheta` C++ mode built same evening as the identification-theory discussion, to finally act on it.** $\theta_{\text{smooth}}=(\delta_0,\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ held completely fixed — not merely seeded, never a decision variable NLopt ever touches — only $\gamma$ (all 10) optimized per cell. Mirrors `revgrid_indep`'s two-level work-stealing exactly, minus the four theta dimensions (`InnerParamsRevGridFixedTheta`, `fit_one_revgrid_point_fixedtheta`, `run_revenue_grid_fixedtheta_mode`, CLI `theta=`/`gamma0=` contract). Compiled clean, smoke-tested (2×2 grid, confirmed theta genuinely untouched across cells) before the real launch.

**Design: $\theta$ fixed at the 3D cube's new global minimum** $(\delta_0,\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2)=(3.46356,5.427\times10^{-7},4.3,0.54)$ — the first point in the whole project to clear the hard test. $\gamma_{1..9}$ seeded from the cube's own converged fit at that exact point (already well-optimized for this $\theta$); $\gamma_{10}$ (the revenue moment's own multiplier, no prior estimate) refined via one anchor cell before broadcasting to all 35 cells, no chaining. $R$-grid baselines recomputed fresh at the new $\theta$ (shifted slightly — e.g. $\Delta=0$'s own baseline moved from \$1,700.92 to \$1,701.93). Same $\Delta$ grid and $\pm20\%$/5-point $R$-grid as the original vacuous attempt, to isolate the effect of fixing $\theta$ specifically. Orchestration: `Code/Deconvolution/run-revgrid-fixedtheta-lag_m.sh`.

**Result: still vacuous.** Max $TS=0.45$ (both soft and hard), essentially unchanged from the free-$\theta$ attempt's $0.448$ — **fixing $\theta$ did not fix the vacuous outcome.** This is a genuinely useful negative result: the $\lambda$-wandering diagnosed the morning before was real and worth fixing on its own methodological grounds (it matches AK2020's actual practice, and the resulting theory work explained the whole project's $\lambda$-identification saga), but it was never actually *the* mechanism producing an all-pass grid at this $R$-grid width — $\gamma$ alone, with 10 genuinely free dimensions, is flexible enough to tilt the sample's mean to match almost any candidate $R$ within $\pm20\%$ of baseline, with or without $\theta$ free. The original "$R$-grid too narrow" diagnosis (made before the $\theta$-fixing detour even started) was correct all along and remains the actual blocker. Real, useful byproduct: each cell ran 2–3$\times$ faster than the 14-parameter version (12–35 min vs. 15–115 min) — fixing $\theta$ was worth doing regardless, just not sufficient by itself.

**Widened follow-up launched autonomously, same night, while the user slept** — explicitly anticipated and requested beforehand ("hopefully this time, we can bound revenue and fine grid in a second pass... leave the grid running in the back"). Re-ran the quadratic TS-vs-\%-deviation extrapolation on this cleaner, theta-fixed data: now consistent across *all* 7 $\Delta$ legs (the earlier free-$\theta$ version had one leg, $\Delta=+1\%$, too noisy to extrapolate at all) — needs roughly $\pm133\%$ to $\pm168\%$ deviation to cross the rejection threshold, mildly increasing toward the tax-increase side. Widened $R$-grid to baseline$\times\{-0.5,0.25,1.0,1.75,2.5\}$, deliberately including a negative $R$ candidate at the low end (checked the code first: the $R$-moment is a plain subtraction, $R_i/pgdp_i-R$, no positivity assumption anywhere, safe to test). Reused the just-completed run's own converged anchor $\gamma$ directly as the seed (no need to re-solve — it already fits the grid's center well, and NM re-optimizes every cell regardless). Orchestration: `Code/Deconvolution/run-revgrid-fixedtheta-wide-lag_m.sh`. Data lands in `Code/Products/1291-revgrid-fixedtheta-wide-lag_m-combined.csv` — check this first next session.

**Result, landed the same night: finally a genuine, non-vacuous confidence set — and an honest, important limitation alongside it.** For 6 of the 7 $\Delta$'s, the two outermost candidates ($-50\%$/$+150\%$ of baseline) now decisively reject (TS up to 23 against the $\chi^2_{10,.95}=18.3$ threshold) while the inner three pass, giving real finite intervals for the first time this whole counterfactual effort: roughly $[440,3100]$ at $\Delta=-4\%$ narrowing only slightly to $[417,2918]$ at $\Delta=+0.1\%$. $\Delta=+1\%$ alone still passes everywhere even at $\pm150\%$ (TS only 13.2–13.3) — needs an even wider grid to bound at all.

**The catch, and it's a real one: these intervals overlap almost completely across every adjacent $\Delta$.** $\Delta=-1\%$'s $[437,3061]$ and $\Delta=0\%$'s $[426,2978]$ are nearly indistinguishable; $\Delta=0\%$ vs. $\Delta=+0.1\%$ likewise. **Under this formal test, the headline's point-differences — a 1% cut buying +3.51%, a 0.1% increase costing -3.13% — are not statistically distinguishable from each other.** The confidence sets for mean real revenue are simply too wide, relative to the (genuinely present, and probably genuinely real) point-estimate gaps between adjacent $\Delta$'s, for this specific auxiliary-$R$-moment test to tell them apart. Not a bug or a design mistake — a genuine finding about the limits of what this test-inversion approach can currently establish, consistent with this project's own standing view that set-identification is an acceptable, sometimes more honest, deliverable rather than something to route around.

**Left as an open decision for the user, not resolved autonomously**: whether to report the wide-CI result honestly as the finding (the pure-simulation headline numbers stand as point estimates/comparative statics, just not yet backed by a sharp formal interval), or to look for ways to sharpen the test itself (a different/tighter auxiliary moment, more MCMC draws per cell, or reconsidering exactly what population target the test should be pinning down). This is a substantive scientific judgment call, not a mechanical next step, so no further grid was launched past this point without the user's input.

**Not yet done:** this finding is pure forward-simulation off one fixed operating point, not run through the joint $(\Delta,R)$ test-inversion grid (`revgrid`) — no confidence set attaches to $\Delta\approx-0.20/-0.25$ yet, only a point-estimate shape. Building a marginal-revenue-vs-$\Delta$ plot for presentation, and eventually relaunching `revgrid` under the no-$\eta$ mechanism to get real inference on this region, remain open. Recorded in CLAUDE.md's Counterfactual section (new paragraph following the linear-decomposition tollgate) and in memory (`project_counterfactual_status.md`).

## `lag_2_cal_W`'s coarse $\lambda$-grid confirms real identification even for the weaker instrument, but doesn't cleanly resolve into one basin yet (2026-09-10)

**Same recipe as lag_m's coarse grid — same $\lambda$ range ($1.628\times10^{-8}$ to $8.412\times10^{-7}$, 10 log-spaced points), same-point independent seeding, but seeded from lag_m's own best fit** ($\hat\delta_0=3.459,\hat\delta_1=4.315,\hat\delta_2=0.541,\hat\gamma$ as converged there) rather than a fresh center refit — the user's own instruction, reusing a validated result rather than re-deriving one from scratch.

**Result: real, non-flat rejection everywhere except two points — but those two points don't bracket a contiguous region, they're isolated.** $\lambda=1.628\times10^{-8}$ passes (TS$_{\text{soft}}=5.1$, landing in the lag_m-consistent basin $\hat\delta_1\approx4.6,\hat\delta_2\approx0.65$) and $\lambda=5.427\times10^{-7}$ passes as the grid's own minimum (TS$_{\text{soft}}=0$, $\hat L_n=0.000401$, a different basin $\hat\delta_1\approx7.5,\hat\delta_2\approx0.97$) — but the six λ's strictly between them all reject decisively (TS 19.7–66.6, three of them landing in yet a third basin with much smaller $\hat\delta_1\approx0.7$–$1.5$), and the top point ($8.412\times10^{-7}$) rejects hardest of all (TS=1194, $\hat\delta_1\approx23$).

**Checked explicitly whether this is a convergence artifact before treating it as a real finding: it isn't.** Every one of the 10 points shows `convergence=4` (NLopt's `XTOL_REACHED`) on BOTH the initial pass and the warm-started refinement pass — a genuine stopping-criterion convergence, not `5`/`6` (hit `maxeval`/`maxtime`, cut off early) or a negative failure code. Despite every point being seeded identically from the exact same lag_m-consistent starting values, Nelder-Mead's search still wandered into three different basins depending on λ — this instrument's likelihood surface is evidently more prone to carrying the search away from a good seed than lag_m's is, not an artifact of insufficient search budget.

**User's own framing of the takeaway, worth keeping verbatim: `lag_2_cal_W` was already known to be the weaker/more unstable instrument (see the earlier "$\omega^*$ unstable in a way that matters" finding) — the win here is that even for this instrument, $\lambda$ still shows a genuinely bounded rejection pattern once $\eta$ is removed, not a flat surface.** One caveat worth being precise about: the passing point at $\lambda=1.628\times10^{-8}$ sits exactly at the grid's own lower theoretical edge (the same $M^*$-distribution-derived floor used for lag_m, "nothing is evasion") — so it's a pass at the boundary of the tested domain, not an interior lower turnaround; it doesn't yet establish where a true lower CI bound would sit if the domain could be pushed lower (it physically can't — this is the real floor).

**Follow-up: built a side-by-side comparison plot (`Code/Deconvolution/1278-lambdagrid-lagm-vs-lag2W-plot.R` → `Paper/images/1278-lambdagrid-lagm-vs-lag2W.png`) to decide whether a finer grid was worth running.** Clarified which pass is which: the floor pass ($\lambda=1.628\times10^{-8}$) is structurally guaranteed (below the floor $\lambda$ affects nobody's fit, so the surface is flat there by construction, not evidence of a real second identified region); the genuine finding is the interior optimum at $\lambda=5.427\times10^{-7}$, which shows real curvature on both sides — three near-miss neighbors just below it ($1.457\times10^{-7}$ at TS=19.7, $2.258\times10^{-7}$ at TS=18.3, $3.501\times10^{-7}$ at TS=18.2, all very close to the 16.92 cutoff) before a sharp drop to the passing point, then a sharp blow-up just above it ($8.412\times10^{-7}$, TS=1194, a clearly different bad basin). The near-miss cluster's proximity to the threshold looked, on its face, like the same under-resolved-dip signature that motivated lag_m's own coarse→fine grid — a finer bracketing grid (seeded from $5.427\times10^{-7}$'s own basin rather than lag_m's, since that's the basin that actually fit well here) was proposed.

**Decision: don't run it.** The user's own reasoning, worth keeping verbatim: lag_m and lag_2_cal_W are built from different first-stage residuals ($m^*_{it-1}$ vs. $\tilde{\mathcal W}_{it-2}$), which in turn embed somewhat different production-function-parameter estimates and therefore imply somewhat different $\omega$ distributions for the same firms — so it's not a surprise, and not something a finer grid would fix, that the two instruments' $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ don't match ($\hat\delta_1\approx7.46,\hat\delta_2\approx0.97$ for lag_2_cal_W vs. $\hat\delta_1\approx4.32,\hat\delta_2\approx0.54$ for lag_m). The near-miss cluster's closeness to the threshold is compatible with this being a genuinely separate, somewhat-different-from-lag_m optimum for this instrument, not necessarily evidence of under-resolution. **lag_2_cal_W's investigation is closed at this point: operating point $\lambda=5.427\times10^{-7}$, $(\hat\delta_0,\hat\delta_1,\hat\delta_2)=(10.206,7.460,0.972)$, $\hat L_n=0.000401$.** Data: `Code/Products/1273-lambdagrid-noeta-lag_2_cal_W.csv`.

## Diagnosing why the $(\Delta,R)$ confidence sets are so wide: a new `dvecdiag` mode, the "gamma is frozen at $\Delta=0$" puzzle solved, and the R-moment's SE traced to firm-size heterogeneity (2026-09-12)

**Motivating question, following up on the wide/overlapping $(\Delta,R)$ CIs found the previous session (see "Real $(\Delta,R)$ revgrid relaunched overnight" above): does $\gamma$ actually adjust across candidate $R$'s at a given $\Delta$, and if the answer is "essentially no" at $\Delta=0$, what mechanically lets a wide range of $R$ candidates pass the test?**

**New read-only diagnostic, `dvecdiag` mode (`Code/C-estimator/grid_estimator.cpp`), reuses the existing `compute_dvec_omega_R` internals — dumps the full 10-row $\hat d_n$ vector and each row's own sampling SE ($\sqrt{\text{diag}(\Omega)/n}$) at a GIVEN, already-fixed $(\theta,\gamma,\Delta,R)$, no NLopt call at all.** Smoke-tested against a known combined-CSV row (bit-for-bit $\hat L_n$ match) before use.

**Finding 1: at exactly $\Delta=0$, $\gamma$ is frozen (bit-identical, distance $\sim10^{-10}$) to the shared seed for EVERY tested candidate $R$ — including wide-grid extremes that reject. This is a real structural degeneracy, not an optimizer failure.** Traced to the closed form itself: $e'(\Delta)=\max(0,\frac{e_i}{1+\Delta}+\frac{\Delta}{2\lambda(1+\Delta)})$ collapses to $e'(0)=e_i$ identically (verified directly against `resolve_e_prime_revenue`'s code). At $\Delta=0$ the new R-moment is trying to tilt the exact same object ($e_i$) that three existing moment-set-A rows already pin down — there's no genuinely new identifying direction for $\gamma_{10}$ to exploit there, so NLopt (which DID run ~230–260 evals, `XTOL_REACHED`, not an early exit) correctly finds no improving move. Away from $\Delta=0$ the extra $\Delta/(2\lambda(1+\Delta))$ term is a firm-invariant shift absent from every other moment, which is presumably why $\gamma$ moves normally at every other tested $\Delta$ (confirmed: $\Delta=+0.1\%$, only 0.1pp away, shows normal $\gamma$ movement).

**Finding 2: even where $\gamma$ DOES move a lot (checked at $\Delta=-0.04$, where the $\psi$-multiplier ranges 0.034–0.183 across 5 candidate $R$'s), the achieved implied mean revenue barely moves at all (1764.8–1768.2, a 3-unit range) regardless of which $R$ is being tested.** The large $\gamma$ swings are being spent trading off *other* moments against each other (e.g. $\mathbb E[\varepsilon\cdot e]$ swings from 14.5 to $-0.04$ across the same 5 cells) — not on shifting $E[R]$. The model genuinely tries, and fails, to move its own implied revenue mean.

**Finding 3: the R-moment's own standard error, $\approx559.4$, is essentially IDENTICAL across every cell checked — both $\Delta$'s, frozen-$\gamma$ or wandering-$\gamma$, passing or rejected — and, verified empirically, completely unaffected by MCMC chain length** (`n_keep\in\{300,3000,30000\}$, a 100x range, gives `se10=559.36` to 4 significant figures every time). This settles that `se10` is a **between-firm** quantity (sample variance of each firm's own MCMC-averaged $g_{10}$ across the $n=32{,}232$ firms), not a simulation-noise artifact — no amount of extra MCMC draws will tighten it.

**Conclusion handed to the user: the wide, overlapping confidence sets are a precision/power problem, not a flexibility-of-the-model problem.** Nothing is being "found" by the optimizer to rationalize far-off $R$'s; the R-moment alone just doesn't have enough power, given how much revenue varies by firm size, to sharply pin down $E[R]$.

### Fix, proposed and empirically validated: a control-variate correction using the firm's OWN observed $t1_i/pgdp_i$ — beats industry×year TWFE by ~15x

**User's proposal: reduce the heterogeneity via industry × year fixed effects (different sectors pay different rates, tax policy changed over years).** Checked directly rather than assumed (`/tmp/twfe_check2.R`, using the ACTUAL model-implied $R_i(\Delta)$ from a fresh `revenue_baseline` forward-sim, not a naive $t1/pgdp$ proxy — an early version of this check made exactly that tautological mistake and was caught/redone): industry+year *additive* FE barely helps (SE $559\to508$); the full industry×year *interaction* (318 cells) does real work (SE $559\to223$, R²=0.84) but carries real overfitting risk (100/318 cells have <30 obs, 25 have <10).

**A much better, much cheaper alternative exists: the firm's own already-observed $t1_i/pgdp_i$ (raw sales-tax paid, no model dependence at all) is a near-perfect linear predictor of $R_i(\Delta)$ — R²$\approx$0.999 at both $\Delta=0$ and $\Delta=-0.04$, slope $\hat\beta\approx0.999$ both times.** This is the standard control-variate / regression-adjusted-mean trick (CUPED in the causal-inference literature; TWFE is the same family of idea with a saturated-categorical control instead of a continuous one) — replace $g_{10,i}=R_i/pgdp_i-R$ with $g_{10,i}^{\text{adj}}=(R_i/pgdp_i-\beta c_i)-(R-\beta\mu_c)$, $c_i=t1_i/pgdp_i$, $\beta\approx0.999$ and $\mu_c=\text{mean}(c_i)\approx2507.6$ both **precomputed constants from data, not new NLopt decision variables** (verified algebraically and numerically that this preserves $E[g_{10}^{\text{adj}}]=E[g_{10}]$ exactly — the target $R$ is untouched, only its variance shrinks). Implied SE: $559\to\approx14$, a ~38-41x improvement — roughly 15x better than the industry×year interaction's 2.5x, with zero added dimensionality and none of the small-cell risk. Once $t1_i$ is controlled for, industry×year adds almost nothing further (R² 0.9993→0.9994), confirming it was mostly a noisy proxy for firm size all along.

### Second alternative proposed by the user, implemented and compared head-to-head: test the REVENUE LOSS due to evasion instead of raw R

**Idea: since $t1_i$ (pure firm-size heterogeneity, nothing to do with evasion) is what's swamping $R_i$'s precision, why not target the loss directly — the uncaught-evasion leakage $\text{Loss}_i(\Delta)=\tilde\tau_P\cdot(1-q(e'))\cdot e'$ — which by construction excludes the $M_i\tilde\tau_P$ "legitimate credit" term that carries the same size heterogeneity as $t1_i$?** New output added to `revenue_baseline` mode (`RevenueResult` struct extended with `Loss_nominal`/`Loss_real`, computed on the same MCMC draws already being generated for $R$ — no extra simulation cost).

**Result, compared head-to-head with the CV-adjusted-$R$ approach at two $\Delta$'s:**

| | $\Delta=0$ | $\Delta=-0.04$ |
|---|---|---|
| Raw $R$: SE | 559.4 | 559.4 |
| CV-adjusted $R$: SE | 14.7 (38x) | 13.6 (41x) |
| Loss: SE | **0.66 (844x)** | **0.14 (4027x)** |
| Loss: mean | 38.0 | 1.04 |
| frac(Loss==0) | 10.4% (≈corner share) | 95.4% |

**Loss wins decisively on absolute SE at both points — but with a real caveat found along the way, not swept under the rug: at $\Delta=-0.04$, 95.4% of firms have Loss exactly 0 (this $\lambda$ makes the per-firm "unclip threshold" $-2\lambda e_i$ tiny, so a 4% cut already clips almost everyone — consistent with the already-documented "Zone 1" flat-MR headline finding).** At that point Loss's distribution is a near-degenerate point-mass-at-zero plus a thin right tail from a handful of extreme evaders — the CLT-based normal approximation underlying the CUE/$\chi^2$ test is on much shakier ground there, even though $n=32{,}232$ is large. At $\Delta=0$ (current policy, no clipping distortion — 89.6% of firms have strictly positive Loss) the distribution is far better-behaved and the 844x SE improvement should be trustworthy.

**Not yet decided which to adopt for the real $(\Delta,R)$ (or $(\Delta,\text{Loss})$) grid — open choice for the user, both now implemented and validated at the single-firm-forward-sim level:** the CV-adjusted-$R$ moment is the "safe," always-well-behaved choice (38-41x, never degenerate since $R_i$ is never literally zero for interior firms); the Loss moment is far sharper (up to 4000x) and more directly on-topic for a tax-evasion paper's headline object, but needs the skewness/point-mass issue watched at $\Delta$'s deep enough into the clipping regime — likely fine near $\Delta=0$, likely risky at $\Delta\lesssim-0.02$ or so for this $\lambda$. A natural next step if Loss is chosen: re-run the same skewness check at a few more $\Delta$'s to map out where the point-mass problem actually starts to bite, rather than assuming it based on just these two points.

## Real $(\Delta,\text{target})$ grids launched for BOTH CV-adjusted-$R$ and Loss, side by side, Δ narrowed to $[-0.05,0.05]$ step $0.01$ (2026-09-12)

**User's decision, given up front rather than picked between the two alternatives: run both moment types head-to-head over the SAME $\Delta$ grid (11 points, $-5\%$ to $+5\%$ in 1pp steps — "only look close to $\Delta=0$"), each with its own coarse-then-fine grid on its own target scale, launched to run unattended overnight.**

**C++ generalized before launch: `row9_mode` (raw/cv/loss) threaded through `moment_g_A_revenue_one` → `firm_chain_R` → `compute_dvec_omega_R` → both `revgrid_fixedtheta` and `dvecdiag`'s CLI dispatch** (`Code/C-estimator/grid_estimator.cpp`). `compute_row9()` is the single new helper computing whichever row-9 definition is asked for; corner firms (tau_P=0) get the right degenerate value automatically (Loss=0, CV/raw both collapse to $t1$-based formulas) since $\tau_{\text{tilde}}=0$ there regardless of mode. Validated against known values before use: `raw` mode reproduces the pre-refactor $\hat L_n$ bit-for-bit (regression check); `cv` mode's SE (14.6995) and `loss` mode's SE (0.68478) both matched the independently-computed R-based numbers from the earlier exploratory check (14.70, ~0.66-0.68) to within MC noise.

**Design, all validated empirically before launch, not assumed:**
- Pooled $\hat\beta=0.998838$, $\mu_c=2507.602596$ for the CV correction, re-estimated across ALL 11 Deltas' forward-sim draws (more robust than the single-$\Delta=0$ estimate from the exploratory session) — R²=0.99916.
- Per-$\Delta$ forward-sim centers/sizing-SEs for both targets, via `revenue_baseline` (already outputs both `R_real` and the newly-added `Loss_real` from the SAME MCMC draws, no extra cost) — `Code/Deconvolution/1294-precompute-cv-loss-centers.R` → `Code/Products/1294-cv-loss-grid-design.csv`. Confirms the R-center collapses from ~1776 ($\Delta=-5\%$) to ~10 ($\Delta=+5\%$) while the Loss-center rises from ~0.7 to ~1690 over the same range — a clean, sensible mirror-image pattern. Also confirms the clipping-regime caveat found earlier: `Loss_frac_zero` is a flat 10.4% (exactly the corner-firm share) for every $\Delta\ge0$, but climbs smoothly to 96.5% by $\Delta=-5\%$ — Loss's distribution is well-behaved for $\Delta\gtrsim-0.02$ and increasingly degenerate below that.
- Two new anchor solves (one per new moment type, $\Delta=0$, gamma1-9 fixed at the cube's converged fit, gamma10 refined from a random $10^{-5}$ guess) — same device used for the original R-moment's own gamma10. CV anchor: 345s wall time (full 12 threads on the single cell), converged (`conv=4`, 603 iters), $\hat L_n=0.000155$.
- Coarse grid: 5 points per $(\Delta,\text{type})$ at center$\,\pm\,$SE$\times\{-6,-3,0,3,6\}$ (mirrors the original successful R-grid design), 110 cells total.
- Adaptive fine grid, no human check-in: `Code/Deconvolution/1295-make-fine-grid.R` computes $TS_{\text{soft}}=2n(\hat L_n-\hat L_{n,\min})$ (global min over that moment type's WHOLE combined grid — same convention as `1263-stage2-revgrid-plot.R`/the original R-grid's analyzed CSVs), finds the reject→pass bracket on each side of every $\Delta$'s coarse points, and linearly interpolates (in TS space) one candidate per bracket targeting the $\chi^2_{10,.95}$ crossing. A side with no bracket (every coarse point already passes) is left explicitly OPEN rather than fabricated — verified correct against a synthetic test case by hand before trusting it on real data.

**Two real bugs caught and fixed during pre-launch review, before any of the 110+ real cells were run — worth remembering for any future shell-orchestrated grid on this machine:**
1. **Locale bug**: this machine's default locale is `es_ES.UTF-8` (comma decimal separator), which silently broke every `awk` numeric filter (`$1+0==d+0` always evaluated `0==0`, matching every row regardless of the target Δ) — caught by testing the filter in isolation before launch, not discovered mid-run. Fix: `export LC_ALL=C` at the top of the orchestration script.
2. **Path bug**: an early draft of the orchestration script used relative `cd ..`/`cd Code/C-estimator` chains that landed one directory off (and separately double-prefixed `Code/Code/Products/...` in one spot) — caught by re-reading the script line by line after noticing the locale bug, not by running it and failing. Rewrote the whole script to use absolute paths throughout (`$ROOT`, `$PRODUCTS`, `$BIN`) rather than tracking relative `cd` state across ~130 lines — a more robust pattern for any future long unattended script, not just this one.

**Orchestration**: `Code/Deconvolution/run-cv-loss-grid.sh` (coarse → combine → fine-grid generation → fine → combine → final plot, all in one script, `set -e`, no chaining of gamma across grid cells — every cell seeded from its own moment type's anchor). Final report script: `Code/Deconvolution/1296-cv-loss-final-plot.R` (extends `1293`'s AK2020-style template to both moment types side by side). **Launched detached (nohup+disown) once both anchors converged — check `Code/Products/1296-cv-bounds-final.csv` and `Paper/images/1296-{cv,loss}-standard-ci.png` next session; log at `Code/Products/1294-cv-loss-grid-launch.log`.**

**Interim result, coarse-only, checked mid-run: CV-adjusted R's coarse grid bracketed cleanly at all 11 Deltas (3 of 5 points pass, both extremes reject, every single Delta) — no "open" sides at all.** Bounds are dramatically tighter than the old raw-R grid (~80-110 COP wide per Delta vs. the old ~2,500+ COP-wide bands), and — the actual headline win — **adjacent Deltas are now visibly non-overlapping** (e.g. Delta=0%'s [1656,1745] vs Delta=+1%'s [1310,1402]), unlike the old raw-R grid where adjacent Deltas' CIs were "nearly the same interval." Saved as `Paper/images/1294-cv-coarse-interim.png` (explicitly labeled interim/coarse-only, not the final combined result). Shape matches the documented revenue collapse exactly (flat -5% to -1%, sharp fall from 0% onward).

**Loss's coarse grid came back mostly inconclusive at the same relative (±6 naive-SE) spread: only 3 of 22 possible brackets found** (lower side only, at Delta=+3%,+4%,+5%) — every other Delta/side is OPEN, meaning the fitted-gamma SE for Loss is larger than the naive forward-sim SE estimate away from the positive-Delta extreme. Not a bug — just means Loss's grid, sized off the same naive-SE heuristic that worked well for CV, wasn't wide enough for Loss specifically.

**User's decision (2026-09-12 night): go with CV-adjusted R, not Loss, as the moment for this counterfactual.** Explicit instruction: do NOT kill the already-running process — let the remaining Loss fine-grid cells (3 cells, ~20-25 min extra) finish rather than risk destabilizing a live unattended script for a small time saving; the Loss output just won't be used. Once the 11 CV fine batches finish (Stage 3's first loop), the plan is to build the final CV-only view directly (same method as the interim check) rather than wait for Stage 4, which is gated on both moment types' fine stages completing.

**Caught before the user went to bed: the pipeline had NOT been wrapped in `caffeinate`.** A stray `caffeinate -i -t 300` (5-minute timed, unrelated/incidental) was the only thing on the system at that moment — not something to rely on for an overnight run. Fixed: `caffeinate -dims -w 64070` launched detached, bound to the orchestration script's own PID (so it automatically stops preventing sleep once the pipeline finishes, no manual cleanup needed). Confirmed the machine is a Mac mini (not a laptop), so there's no lid-close-forces-sleep risk on top of this — `pmset -g` confirmed `sleep 0` after binding. Pipeline had been running ~11h at that point (much longer than the original ~10-14h estimate suggested for this stage — the coarse stage alone took the bulk of it, consistent with the per-batch timing observed: 25-34 min/batch × 22 coarse batches).

**Correction, same day: the "floor pass is structurally guaranteed/uninformative" claim above was wrong — checked directly against lag_m's own grid and falsified.** The user asked whether lag_m's grid tested the same floor value; it does ($\lambda=1.628\times10^{-8}$ appears in `1270-lambdagrid-lag_m-allcombined.csv`), and lag_m **rejects** it decisively (TS$_{\text{soft}}=438.6$, $\hat\delta_1=43.2,\hat\delta_2=7.65$) — the opposite of lag_2_cal_W's comfortable pass at the identical $\lambda$. So the floor is not automatically flat/uninformative regardless of the data: with $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ free at every grid point, whether *some* cost-curvature shape can still fit acceptably once $\lambda$'s effect is negligible is a genuine, instrument-specific empirical question — lag_m's data reject negligible $\lambda$ outright (a real lower identification bound baked into that instrument), lag_2_cal_W's don't (this instrument alone can't distinguish its own best interior fit at $\lambda=5.427\times10^{-7}$ from $\lambda\to0$). This is a sharper and more precise statement of "lag_2_cal_W is the weaker instrument" than the earlier basin-instability framing: it's a checkable gap in identification, not just noisier optimization behavior. Doesn't change the decision not to chase a finer grid (that was about $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ disagreement, a separate question) — only corrects why the floor pass shouldn't be read as evidence of a second identified region.

## $\delta_0$ checked for boundary-hitting across the lambdagrid, deltagrid, and 3D cube — none found, reassuring cross-check on the $\lambda$-identification theory (2026-09-13)

**Motivating question, following directly from the "why $\lambda$ is structurally harder to identify" entry above: if the 3$\times$3 psi-only linear system were ill-conditioned or degenerate at some $\lambda$, the optimizer might be forced to push $\hat\delta_0$ to its box bound ($\pm60$, `DELTA_BOUND` in `grid_estimator.cpp`) to compensate, rather than landing on a genuine interior solution — which would undercut the claim that $(\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ are cleanly, separately pinned down given $\lambda$, and that $\lambda$'s own discrimination is coming honestly from the $h'_{\text{bounded}}\cdot\varepsilon$ score moment rather than from some hidden boundary-driven slack.** Checked directly against the raw output of all three grids rather than assumed.

**Lambdagrid (16 pts, no-$\eta$, `1270-lambdagrid-lag_m-allcombined.csv`, $\hat\delta_0$ pulled from the underlying shard/fine files since the combined file drops it):** $\hat\delta_0$ ranges from $-0.21$ to $44.19$ across the full $\lambda$ range tested ($1.628\times10^{-8}$ to $0.01613$). The three soft-passing points ($\lambda=2.258\times10^{-7},3.501\times10^{-7},5.427\times10^{-7}$) cluster tightly at $\hat\delta_0\approx3.49$–$3.54$; the decisively-rejecting points scatter much higher (up to 44.19). Nowhere near $\pm60$.

**Deltagrid (25 pts, $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ fixed on the $5\times5$ diagnostic grid, $\lambda$ and $\delta_0$ free, `1283-deltagrid-lag_m-combined.csv`):** $\hat\delta_0$ ranges from $-5.99$ to $14.02$, moving smoothly and monotonically as $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ move across the grid (expected, since $\delta_0$ absorbs their location shift). At $(\delta_1,\delta_2)=(4.3,0.54)$ — the good basin — $\hat\delta_0=3.43$.

**3D cube (27 pts, `1288-cube-lag_m-combined.csv`):** $\hat\delta_0$ ranges from $-1.96$ to $8.97$. At the global minimum ($\lambda=5.427\times10^{-7},\delta_1=4.3,\delta_2=0.54$): $\hat\delta_0=3.464$ — matching the lambdagrid's and deltagrid's own estimates at the same neighborhood to 3 significant figures.

**Conclusion: $\hat\delta_0$ never approaches $\pm60$ in any of the 78 cells checked across the three grids, and it's not merely "not at the bound" — it's stable and cross-validated, with three independent profiling directions (fix $\lambda$; fix $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$; fix all three) converging to the same $\hat\delta_0\approx3.43$–$3.54$ at the same $(\lambda,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ neighborhood.** This confirms, rather than merely asserts, the identification-theory entry above: the psi-only $3\times3$ linear system is well-conditioned and genuinely pins down $(\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ given $\lambda$, so the work of discriminating between candidate $\lambda$'s really is coming from the $h'$ score moment's nonlinear leverage — not from an optimizer quietly parking $\delta_0$ at a wall to paper over a degenerate fit at some $\lambda$'s. No follow-up action needed; kept as a documented reassurance check.

## CV-R grid extended to 14 Deltas; hard-test properly bisected; a real self-correction on the "-6%/-7% reversal" claim (2026-09-13)

**Extension, per user's explicit request: added Delta=-0.07,-0.06,+0.005 to the CV-adjusted-R grid, same procedure as the original 11-point run (coarse+adaptive-fine, soft-targeted).** Global min Lhat unchanged (still 0.0001429 at Delta=+0.03), confirming consistency with the original grid rather than a different local reference. Result: Delta=+0.5% gives a closed soft-CI [1472,1590], cleanly non-overlapping with both 0% [1646,1762] and +1% [1298,1420]. Delta=-0.06 closes [1718,1830]; Delta=-0.07's upper side is OPEN under soft (widest tested candidate still passes). Plot: `Code/Deconvolution/1298-cv-14delta-soft-plot.R` -> `Paper/images/1298-cv-14delta-soft.png`.

**Step 2: hard-test-specific fine-tuning, 11 Deltas (-7% to +2%, including +0.5%) x up to 2 points = 22 cells.** New bracket-finder `Code/Deconvolution/1299-make-hard-fine-grid.R` (simpler than the soft version -- hard's threshold is absolute, `TS_hard=2n*Lhat<=chi2(10,.95)`, no min-subtraction needed, so no dependency on a global minimum that could shift). Orchestration: `run-hard-finetune.sh`. **Validated the fix worked: hard-test bounds are now properly NESTED inside the soft-test bounds at all 14 Deltas** (checked explicitly -- the earlier crude linear-interpolation "projection" had violated this in 5/11 cases, which is what motivated running the real bisection in the first place).

**Self-correction, worth remembering: an unverified claim ("the -6% vs -7% overlap reversal is just optimizer noise") turned out to be wrong when actually checked.** User pushed back and asked for the basis -- checking the underlying data showed the real explanation is bisection-RESOLUTION asymmetry, not fitting noise: at Delta=-6%, the single bisection guess (R=1731.10) landed just barely on the REJECT side (TS_hard=19.09 vs threshold 18.307), so the reported bound fell back to the next already-tested point (R=1744.42, which happened to sit very close to Delta=0's own bound, making the overlap look artificially tiny at 0.21). At Delta=-7%, the bisection guess (R=1741.92) happened to land on the PASS side, close to the true crossing. Interpolating properly between each Delta's actual reject/pass bracket gives a clean, monotonic overlap trend with Delta=0 instead: -5%:24.0, -6%:11.3 (not 0.21), -7%:4.1 (not 2.71) -- no reversal at all once resolution quality is equalized. **Lesson: don't attribute an unexplained pattern to "noise" without checking the underlying bracket data first -- this project's own standing feedback ("verify before concluding") applied directly here and the user was right to ask.**

**Final answer on tax cuts vs. baseline, hard test: none of -1% through -7% achieve full statistical separation from Delta=0, but the trend is a clean, decelerating approach with no reversal.** Extrapolating (with appropriately different confidence levels): Delta=-6.5% is genuine INTERPOLATION between two already-tested points -- true crossing estimated around R=1737-1738, still ~7 units short of Delta=0's upper bound (1744.63), so likely still overlapping, not yet separating. Delta=-10% is EXTRAPOLATION well beyond tested data (curve shape defined by only two deceleration steps, -12.7 then -7.2) -- naive continuation puts the true crossing around Delta=-8%, meaning -10% would likely show genuine but modest separation -- flagged explicitly as a low-confidence guess, not a verified result; would need real grid points at -8%/-9%/-10% to know for sure.

**Final plot, all 14 Deltas, hard test, with a gray reference band for Delta=0's own interval (new design element, makes "distinguishable from baseline" visually immediate without the reader doing arithmetic): `Code/Deconvolution/1299-cv-14delta-hard-plot.R` -> `Paper/images/1299-cv-14delta-hard.png`.** Increases (Delta>=+0.5%) separate cleanly and immediately from the gray band; every tested cut's lower bound stays inside it.

## Crossing point found: Delta=-8% is the smallest tax cut distinguishable from baseline (2026-09-13)

**User-designed sequential search, more compute-efficient than a fixed grid: since a candidate Delta's separation from baseline is monotone in the magnitude of the cut (in this range), testing a SINGLE point per candidate Delta suffices.** Insight: because cuts only raise revenue here, a candidate Delta has fully separated from Delta=0 iff a single test point at R=1744.6285220744 (exactly Delta=0's own hard-test upper bound) gets REJECTED there -- if rejected, that Delta's whole passing interval must sit above 1744.63 (given the monotonic trend); if it still passes, they still overlap. One optimized point (~10-30 min at 12 threads) per candidate instead of a full 5+-point grid (~35-50 min).

**Algorithm** (`Code/Deconvolution/run-crossing-search.sh`): start at Delta=-10% (the "safe/far" end). If it separates, walk BACK toward zero in 1% steps (-9%, -8%; -7% already known from the full grid to not separate) until finding the first point that does NOT separate -- brackets the crossing to 1pp. If -10% does NOT separate, escalate to -15%, confirm it separates, then walk back from there. Implemented as a single self-branching bash script (no human-in-the-loop between steps), using `bc -l` for the TS_hard-vs-threshold comparison, validated against a known point (Delta=0, R=1700.53, expected TS_hard~10.00) before trusting it.

**Result: -10% separates (TS=29.08) -> -9% separates (TS=24.86) -> -8% separates, but narrowly (TS=19.88 vs threshold 18.307) -> -7% (already known) does not separate. Crossing = Delta=-0.08.** Matches the earlier decelerating-trend extrapolation (~-8%) almost exactly -- good validation that extrapolation, despite being explicitly flagged as low-confidence at the time, was sound.

**Full standard grid then run at Delta=-0.08** (`run-m08-full-grid.sh`: coarse 5pts -> soft-targeted fine (merged with the full existing dataset for a consistent global min) -> hard-targeted fine, same pattern as every other Delta in this grid) for real bounds, not just the single-point separation check. Result: HARD-CI $[1750.70, 1838.63]$, cleanly separated from Delta=0's $[1666.30, 1744.63]$ (gap=6.07, confirmed via the full 8-point-tested grid at this Delta, not just the earlier 1-point check). Soft test's upper side at both -0.08 and -0.07 is OPEN (widest tested candidate still passes) -- not pursued further since hard is the adopted headline test.

**Final integrated dataset: 15 Delta points, `Code/Products/1300-cv-16delta-final.csv`** (filename says 16 for historical reasons, actually 15 unique Deltas: -8% through +5%). Final plot: `Code/Deconvolution/1300-cv-15delta-hard-plot.R` -> `Paper/images/1300-cv-15delta-hard.png`, with a corrected caption reporting the confirmed crossing (an earlier draft's caption, copied from the 14-Delta version, was stale and said the crossing wasn't yet pinned -- caught and fixed before treating the plot as final).

**This closes out the counterfactual's core empirical question for now: increases are cleanly distinguishable from baseline starting at +0.5%; cuts require going out to -8% (roughly 8x the pure-simulation headline's own suggested "optimal" cut of ~-20% to -25%'s much SMALLER analog, ~-1pp) before the data can statistically rule out "no effect."** Next step (not started): write this into the paper/slides -- `600-opt-tax.qmd`'s Counterfactual section still only has the old wide/vacuous-CI-era discussion.

## Economic mechanism behind the cut/increase asymmetry worked out; convex/concave detection curvature solved analytically; a revenue-floor robustness check designed (not launched) (2026-09-13)

**User asked directly: is the "everyone jumps by a constant factor on the increase side, gradual staggering on the cut side" pattern an economic mechanism or just a modeling artifact?** Worked through via the closed form $e'(\Delta)=e_i/(1+\Delta)+\Delta/(2\lambda(1+\Delta))$: the common intercept has no firm-specific terms in it at all, and since $\hat\lambda$ is tiny, $1/(2\lambda)\approx920{,}000$ dwarfs typical $e_i$ (median 370-840), so the intercept swamps heterogeneity on the increase side; on the cut side the same intercept (negative) staggers firms into the zero floor in order of their own $e_i$ (small firms clipped first), which is the "gradual jumping out of evasion" the user described. Concluded: this is a modeling-artifact consequence of linear $q$ + linear $\kappa$ jointly, not a general economic law.

**User self-corrected this to a sharper, verified statement: it's not linearity per se, it's linearity *combined with $\lambda$ being small*.** Rewriting as $e'(\Delta)=[e_i+\Delta/(2\lambda)]/(1+\Delta)$ makes this a horse race between $e_i$ and $\Delta/(2\lambda)$ — for $\lambda$ large enough the heterogeneity term would dominate even under exact linearity. Verified quantitatively: the threshold $\lambda\sim\Delta/(2e_i)$ evaluates to $\sim5\times10^{-6}$ for $\Delta=1\%,e_i=1000$, about 10x above $\hat\lambda=5.427\times10^{-7}$ — confirming the estimated enforcement intensity, not the functional form alone, is what drives the result. Also confirmed the "refund compounding" interpretation: $E[e'(\Delta)]=E[e_i]/(1+\Delta)+\Delta/(2\lambda(1+\Delta))$, and the intercept term is literally the average extra credit claimed/paid out economy-wide.

**Convex and concave detection curvature solved analytically (not estimated) to check whether the uniform-jump result is robust to $q$'s functional form.**
- Convex $q=(\lambda e)^k$: $e=\frac1\lambda\left(\frac{1-C/(\tau\rho_t)}{1+k}\right)^{1/k}$ — nests the linear closed form exactly at $k=1$ (verified). For $k>1$ this is a concave transform of the benefit term, dampening high-$e_i$ firms' response relative to low-$e_i$ firms.
- Concave $q=1-e^{-\lambda e}$: FOC reduces to $(1-\lambda e)e^{-\lambda e}=C/(\tau\rho_t)$, solved via a substitution $u=1-\lambda e$ landing exactly on the Lambert $W$ function's defining equation ($ue^u=z$): $e=[1-W_0(K\exp(1))]/\lambda$, $K=C/(\tau\rho_t)$. Consistency check passed: $W_0(\exp(1))=1$ exactly, matching the already-documented ceiling $\lambda e<1$ for this functional form.
- **Both break the uniform-shift result** — the mapping from $\Delta$ to $e$ is genuinely nonlinear in the firm's own type in both cases, so $\Delta$ and firm heterogeneity stop being additively separable. Conclusion: the uniform-jump property is a knife-edge feature specific to linear-$q$-AND-linear-$\kappa$, not a robust prediction of "simple" functional forms generally.
- **Identification caveat raised and accepted**: the observed right-skew of $e$ doesn't cleanly distinguish convex from concave $q$, since that skew is confounded with the (already right-skewed-generating) distribution of firm type $C(\omega,\psi)$ — matching skewness alone isn't valid identification for $q$'s curvature.

**Cap idea evolved through two iterations, the second one caught by the user as more economically correct.**
1. First proposal (mine): cap the evaded credit's tax value directly at $t1_i$ (can't extract more fraud-credit than output tax paid): $e'_{\text{capped}}=\min(e', t1_i/\tilde\tau_P(\Delta))$.
2. **User caught the flaw**: this still allows $R_i<0$ (a net cash refund) because it doesn't net out the LEGITIMATE credit on true materials $M_i$ first. User's own framing, kept close to verbatim: going from "pay reduced tax" to "the government owes me money" is a much bigger, more suspicious jump than reducing evasion all the way to zero tax paid — the natural constraint is $R_i\ge0$, not a cap on $e'$ in isolation.
3. **Corrected formalization, proven (not assumed) equivalent to a "rational" cap on $e'$ itself**: since $R_i(e')=t1_i-\tilde\tau_P[M_i+(1-q(e'))e']$ is strictly monotonically decreasing in $e'$ over the model's existing domain $e'<1/(2\lambda)$ (because uncaught evasion $e'-\lambda e'^2$ is strictly increasing there — the model's own FOC ceiling already guarantees this domain is never exceeded), $\max(0,R_i(\Delta))$ gives the *exact same number* as would come from a firm rationally capping its own $e'$ at the point where $R_i=0$. This means the robustness check needs **zero changes to the FOC or the closed form for $e'(\Delta)$** — just wrap the existing revenue formula in $\max(0,\cdot)$. Much simpler than the quadratic-inequality cap the first (flawed) proposal would have required.

**Scoped as a future robustness-check appendix figure, NOT launched yet (user explicitly deferred it pending a conversation with Nail Kashaev about the fixed-$\theta$ counterfactual design more broadly).** Plan: new C++ `row9_mode` (`cvcap`), needs its own anchor solve (gamma10 refined fresh, same device as every other new moment type), full coarse+fine grid (hard test only, matching the adopted headline — not soft) at $\Delta\in\{+2\%,+4\%,+8\%\}$ only (the cut side is mechanically untouched by an upper-side cap, so no need to rerun it). Estimated cost ~4.5-6 hours if/when run. **User's own stated expectation, worth recording verbatim for later comparison: fairly confident that even letting $\theta$ float freely (starting from the current optimum) rather than fixing it, the CV correction would reproduce similar results — hopeful, not yet verified.**

**Next steps (not started): (1) get Nail Kashaev's feedback on fixing $\theta$ for the counterfactual (the design already has a literature-check precedent in AK2020's own code, per CLAUDE.md, but the user wants a second opinion); (2) write the settled 15-Delta hard-test result and the mechanism discussion into `Quarto-Slides/sections/600-opt-tax.qmd`'s Counterfactual section, which currently only reflects the much older pure-simulation-only, no-confidence-set era.**

## Tim Conley's proposed naive-GMM fix (`1201-MSM.R`): two real bugs found and fixed, then decisive rejection for both instruments — independent empirical confirmation of the standing MSM-rejection argument (2026-09-13/14)

**Context.** From the 2026-09-11 committee meeting: Tim Conley (spatial-inference specialist, not an ELVIS expert) was skeptical of ELVIS on preference grounds and couldn't be talked out of it in the room. He proposed a concrete, checkable alternative instead of a philosophical objection: simulate from the joint distribution of $(\varepsilon,\omega)$, compute the GMM objective at a parameter guess using the efficient (inverse-covariance) weight, and use the fact that this object has a $\chi^2$ limiting distribution to test the fit. The user implemented this directly in `Code/Deconvolution/1201-MSM.R`.

**First pass (4 moments, exactly identified): confirmed the textbook Hansen (1982) J-test requires $d_g>d_\theta$, which this design didn't have.** With $g(\theta)=(\psi,\psi\omega,\psi\omega^2,\varepsilon h'_{\text{bounded}})$ against 4 parameters $(\lambda,\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2)$, $d_g=d_\theta=4\Rightarrow d_g-d_\theta=0$: an exactly-identified GMM system can (generically) drive its own moments to zero regardless of whether the model is true, so $\chi^2_0$ is a degenerate point mass at zero and no valid test exists — a standard textbook fact (Hansen 1982; Newey & McFadden 1994), not a flaw in Tim's reasoning, which implicitly assumed the usual overidentified setup. Confirmed this numerically by recomputing the objective from the exact same seeded draws: both instruments converged cleanly (NLopt `XTOL_REACHED`) but landed at economically implausible fits (lag_m: $\hat\delta_1=-0.069$, wrong-signed; $\hat\lambda$ 25–170$\times$ ELVIS's own operating range).

**User added a genuine overidentifying moment, $\psi\cdot\ln M$ (one of ELVIS's own moment-set-A rows), making $d_g=5>d_\theta=4$ — reviewed for bugs before rerunning, per the user's request.**

**Bug 1 (pre-existing, not introduced by the user's edit, but load-bearing once a real test exists): the CUE quadratic form was computed in the wrong basis.** The code projected the moment vector and weight matrix onto the retained eigenvectors of $\Omega$ (`d2 <- t(A)%*%dvec`, `Omega_inv <- solve(t(A)%*%Omega%*%A)`) but then paired `Omega_inv` with the raw, un-rotated `dvec` instead of the projected `d2` in the final quadratic form. Dimensionally legal whenever no eigenvalue is trimmed, but mathematically wrong unless $\Omega$ is already diagonal in the original coordinate order. Confirmed numerically on a synthetic 4-variable example: correct $\text{dvec}'\Omega^{-1}\text{dvec}=0.0518$, the code as written returned $0.0439$ (~15% error there); worse in the actual application, where $\Omega$'s eigenvalues span 3+ orders of magnitude (1584.8 to 0.22 in the exactly-identified diagnostic). **Fix: use `d2` in place of `dvec` on the final line.**

**Bug 2 (also pre-existing): the moment covariance pooled all $n_{\text{firms}}\times S$ stacked rows as i.i.d.** Rows sharing a firm share every covariate ($M^*$, $\mathcal V$, $\tilde{\mathcal W}$, $\tau_P$) and differ only in the resampled $\varepsilon_s$ — not independent draws. Pooling them mixes genuine between-firm sampling variance with pure simulation noise from resampling $\varepsilon$; the asymptotically correct object to pair with $n=n_{\text{firms}}$ is the cross-firm covariance of each firm's own $S$-draw average (Pakes & Pollard 1989; McFadden 1989 SMM asymptotics — $S$ integrates out $\varepsilon$ by simulation, it is not additional real sample size). **Fix: `build_moments()` now collapses to one row per firm via `rowsum()` before `Omega`/`dvec` are computed.** Separately confirmed the user's own choice of $n=n_{\text{firms}}$ (not $n_{\text{firms}}\times S$) was already correct — only the accompanying code comment was stale. Also made `df_test` self-computing (`d_g_eff - length(par)`) instead of hardcoded, so it survives future eigenvalue-trimming or moment-count changes.

**Operational mishap, worth remembering for any future unattended run: the first relaunch attempt (2026-09-13, 21:08) died silently ~6 minutes in, mid-redraw-loop, with no error printed.** Traced via `pmset -g log` to the Mac entering idle sleep at 21:14:37 — launched via bare `nohup ... &`/`disown` without `caffeinate`, repeating a mistake this project's own log had already documented once before (the overnight CV/Loss grid launch, 2026-09-12). Relaunched wrapped in `caffeinate -dims Rscript ...`, confirmed via `pmset -g assertions` that caffeinate was holding `PreventUserIdleSystemSleep` on behalf of the running `Rscript` PID; completed cleanly both instruments, ~40 min each.

**Result, `Code/Products/1201-stage2-MSM.Rout`: decisive rejection for both instruments, not a marginal call.**

| | $\hat\lambda$ | $\hat\delta_0$ | $\hat\delta_1$ | $\hat\delta_2$ | $\hat\sigma^2_\psi$ | $n_{\text{eff}}$ | $J$-stat | $\chi^2_{1,.95}$ |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| lag_m | 0.004581 | $-8.406$ | 10.904 | 2.389 | 321.6 | 28,691/29,038 | **29,257.8** | 3.841 |
| lag_2_cal_W | $1.624\times10^{-5}$ | $\mathbf{-49.999999}$ | $-25.926$ | $-3.433$ | 81.8 | 28,691/29,038 | **28,961.0** | 3.841 |

Both converged genuinely (NLopt `XTOL_REACHED` on both). Both reject by a factor of ~7,600$\times$ the critical value.

**Diagnosis, several independent red flags pointing the same direction:**
- **lag_2_cal_W's $\hat\delta_0$ lands exactly at its box bound ($-50$)** — precisely the boundary-hitting failure mode this project checked ELVIS's own grids for and found completely absent (see "$\delta_0$ checked for boundary-hitting" entry above, all 78 ELVIS cells landing at $|\hat\delta_0|<45$, nowhere near $\pm60$). A clean, concrete side-by-side: remove ELVIS's entropy-tilt machinery and replace it with naive population sampling, and the boundary-hitting problem that motivated checking for it in the first place shows up immediately.
- **lag_2_cal_W's $\hat\delta_1,\hat\delta_2$ are both negative**, violating the U-shaped-cost sign prediction every ELVIS grid (both instruments) has consistently confirmed.
- **$\hat\sigma^2_\psi$ is enormous for both** (SD $\approx18$ and $9$ on a log-cost residual) — a substantive misfit, not just a statistical technicality.
- **$\hat\lambda$ is 30–13,000$\times$ larger than ELVIS's own soft-CI** ($[2.258\times10^{-7},5.427\times10^{-7}]$ for lag_m) — implies an implausibly tight FOC ceiling ($e<109$ for lag_m's fitted $\lambda$) that most of the actual data would violate, papered over by the numerical floor (`arg <- pmax(1-2\lambda e, 1e-6)`) rather than genuinely satisfied.

**Mechanism — ties directly to the standing MSM-rejection argument already in CLAUDE.md, now confirmed empirically via Tim's own preferred remedy rather than just asserted theoretically.** $\varepsilon_s$ here is drawn from the population/industry-pooled CORP-$\varepsilon$ distribution, filtered only for feasibility ($e>0,M>0$) — not from any given firm's own posterior-consistent conditional distribution given its $(\mathcal V,\tilde{\mathcal W})$. Averaging $g(\theta)$ over such draws estimates a population-marginal object, not what the model's moment conditions require firm-by-firm — the same "missing posterior reweighting" gap already used to reject the original (unconstrained) MSM design. Fixing the two real bugs above and adding a genuine overidentifying restriction didn't change this: the model is still rejected by four orders of magnitude. ELVIS's entropy tilt $\gamma$ is precisely the ingredient that reweights the sampler into the data-consistent conditional distribution this naive design lacks.

**Next step, not started: build a slide answering Tim with this result** for `600-opt-tax.qmd` or a standalone response deck — headline: "implemented your suggestion exactly as proposed, found and fixed two real implementation bugs along the way, it converges cleanly, and it decisively rejects ($J\approx29{,}000$ vs. critical value $3.84$) — independent empirical confirmation that ELVIS's entropy tilt is doing real, necessary work, not an arbitrary methodological preference." Files: `Code/Deconvolution/1201-MSM.R` (fixed script), `Code/Products/1201-stage2-MSM.Rout` (full run log), `Code/Products/1201-stage2-MSM.RData` (saved fits).

### Exactly-identified case rerun under the corrected objective: same conclusion, even before overidentification is possible (2026-09-14)

**Why rerun:** the *original* 4-moment fit (before either bug was found) had used the buggy quadratic form as the actual NLopt objective, not just for post-hoc evaluation — so those numbers (e.g. lag_m's $\hat\delta_1=-0.069$) were never a trustworthy answer to "what does the exactly-identified case alone look like." Added an `include_lnM` CLI toggle to `1201-MSM.R` (default `TRUE`) that reuses the same fixed `build_moments()`/CUE quadratic form but drops the $\psi\cdot\ln M$ moment, reproducing the exactly-identified $d_g=d_\theta=4$ case honestly. Reran both instruments (`include_lnM=FALSE`, `Code/Products/1201-stage2-MSM-exactID.Rout`, ~30 min/instrument, caffeinated).

| | $\hat\lambda$ | $\hat\delta_0$ | $\hat\delta_1$ | $\hat\delta_2$ | $\hat\sigma^2_\psi$ | $\hat L_n$ |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| lag_m | $1.295\times10^{-4}$ | 32.98 | **50.00 (bound)** | 10.47 | 6,415.9 | 1.0216 |
| lag_2_cal_W | $1.629\times10^{-8}$ (bound) | **50.00 (bound)** | 32.46 | 4.63 | 101.5 | 1.0105 |

**This sharpens the argument rather than just repeating it.** $\hat L_n\approx1.0$–$1.02$ here is essentially *identical* to the overidentified (5-moment) fit's $\hat L_n$ (1.0198 lag_m, 1.0094 lag_2_cal_W) — adding the $\psi\cdot\ln M$ moment barely moved the objective at all. In an exactly-identified system, a solution, if one exists, can generically drive $\hat L_n$ to $\approx0$ (four equations, four unknowns); landing at $\hat L_n\approx1$ with a parameter pinned at the $\pm50$ box bound in *both* instruments (lag_m's $\hat\delta_1$; lag_2_cal_W's $\hat\delta_0$, plus $\hat\lambda$ itself at its data-derived floor) means no $(\lambda,\delta_0,\delta_1,\delta_2)$ reconciles even just the first four moments — this was never about the 5th moment being the thing that breaks it. The overidentification test wasn't needed to see the model doesn't fit here; it was needed to attach a formal $p$-value to a failure that's already visible in the exactly-identified case alone.
